Appendix: ASB feedback on the targeted questions in the Commercial credit card interchange fee regulation for Mastercard and Visa networks - Draft Decision and Reasons Paper Commerce Commission ‘targeted question’ 2.1: The relevance of Australia in informing specific elements of our approach, such as issuer incentives and sustaining the ability of smaller card issuers (including smaller banks) or open banking providers to compete, and implied weighted-average outcomes.

The Commerce Commission has been heavily informed by Australia’s approach to interchange regulation, without fully acknowledging the key structural differences between the two markets, nor the impacts created by the imposition of the initial 80 bps cap in July 2017. Since that time, commercial credit cards on issue in Australia has dropped 41.8%, the prevalence of charge cards increased and American Express now commands a ~50% commercial card market share, up from 18.2% in July 20172 .

The Reserve Bank of Australia’s commercial interchange cap has created an uneven playing field between unregulated and regulated schemes which has led to reduced competition.
Beyond this, commercial credit card utilisation varies considerably in New Zealand versus Australia, as illustrated below3
:

Commercial Credit Cards ASB Australia Market
Annual Spend AUD$166,428
Average Balance AUD$12,495
Interest Bearing Balance AUD$2,951

An 80-bps interchange cap in Australia is far less impactful than the same cap in New Zealand due to issuer revenue composition. Australian commercial credit cardholders carry 184% higher interestbearing balances, spend 271% more and on average pay higher fees. As such, the reduced interchange income (via caps) can be more readily absorbed by the wider portfolio economics in Australia than the same cap when applied in New Zealand.

Similar trends are also true within the commercial versus consumer credit card domestic comparison.
of balances are currently incurring interest (revolving) within ASB’s commercial card portfolio. At the national level, that figure is 51.8% for consumer credit cards4 . In summary, commercial credit cards in New Zealand are more reliant on interchange income than consumer products and to a much greater extent when compared to their Australian equivalents. Capping commercial interchange at the same absolute rate in New Zealand will have a disproportionate and more negative impact for issuers in New Zealand that it will/has in Australia.
Commerce Commission ‘targeted question’ 2.2: How regulation in Australia has affected issuer behaviour, including any changes to product offerings, pricing, or investment The Reserve Bank of Australia has had two, soon to be three, regulatory interventions in the market, with the 2017 change likely most relevant for commercial credit cards, as it introduced an 80-bps cap.
2
RBA's 'Review of Merchant Card Payment Costs and Surcharging - consultation paper' 3 RBZ Payment data
4
RBNZ data

5
The initial regulation in 2003 ushered in a period of companion cards, which essentially allowed issuers to offer both three- and four party credit cards to maximise commercial outcomes and cardholder utility. This product construct was a direct result of regulatory asymmetry and should serve as a cautionary example for New Zealand's approach to capping commercial credit cards.
Since 2017, the clear trend in the Australian context has been reduced or capped credit card loyalty earn rates, higher fees (in various forms), higher interest rates and a reduction in product range.
Charge cards have also become more prevalent, offering cardholders reduced functionality compared to a conventional credit card. Commercial credit card propositions have generally seen less change than personal products over the period, primarily due to their higher interchange rates. However, even greater divergence between consumer and commercial markets can be expected once the October 2026 caps on consumer credit cards go live.

ASB notes the 50-bps interchange premium for commercial credit cards (versus consumer) that was codified by the RBA’s latest regulations to promote competition and investment. The Commerce Commission’s 20-bps premium is a clear departure from this approach and suggests that New Zealand’s smaller market, with fewer issuers, needs less competition.

Ultimately, the RBA’s intervention into the Australian market has led to a reduction in the total number of credit cards (consumer and commercial) on issue in Australia, which has fallen by 27.1% since 2017, whilst the population has grown by 16%5
. Such a pronounced reduction in cards on issue represents a material loss of economic value in the form of liquidity and short-term financing for the wider economy.

Commerce Commission ‘targeted question’ 2.3: Key contextual similarities or differences between New Zealand and Australia (including the role of three-party networks) that should affect how these comparisons are applied
The core contextual difference is scale. Australia’s GDP is 7x that of New Zealand’s and materially higher in a per capita basis. The population base is 5x higher, resulting in a Total Addressable Market for an Australian cards issuer that is orders of magnitude higher than in New Zealand. With this scale, Australia can support a wider ecosystem of issuers, from main bank incumbents to new market entrants, such as Airwallex.

The revenue per card for the average commercial credit card in Australia is materially higher than in New Zealand, even with an 80-bps cap on interchange. The combination of a larger market and superior unit level card economics means new and existing card issuers have more scope to compete i.e. they can afford to invest in growth and product augmentation.

The role of American Express is clearly more significant in Australian context. However, we believe the underlying fundamentals are similarly found in New Zealand which enabled American Express to grow so substantially in Australia. We note, American Express has doubled their market share in New Zealand since the implementation of the initial pricing standard in November 20226 . Whilst American
Express remains a smaller player in New Zealand, the Australian example clearly shows rapid and sustained growth is achievable when a regulatory advantage is extended to a market participant.
5
Sources: Reserve Bank of Australia 'Payments Data' and The Australian Bureau of Statistics 6
Source: MWE New Zealand Payment Cards Report

Commerce Commission 'targeted question' 3.1: We are interested in any views stakeholders may have on our approach at step 3 of our methodology, for example on the additional 10 basis-point premium we have applied above domestic personal credit card interchange to reflect the incremental issuer costs and merchant benefits associated with domestic commercial credit cards ASB welcomes an interchange premium above the consumer credit card caps and agrees with the intention underpinning their inclusion. However, we challenge the size of the premium and do not believe it is enough to achieve its stated goal.

The proposed interchange premium does not address ASB's overarching concerns that the proposed interchange caps will reduce competition and limit cardholder access to credit. There is a risk that issuers reassess their market participation and card propositions if the interchange premium is not appropriate.

Higher interchange levels on commercial credit cards (compared to personal) help to foster a sustainable and competitive market. Evidence of this can be found in the growth of credit card balances since the initial pricing standard was introduced in New Zealand in 2022. Since this time, commercial credit card balances have grown 4%. Over the same period consumer credit cards grew only 0.7%.

ASB would consider a single flat interchange cap across all issuer locations (domestic and foreign) and card usage environments (Card Present and Card Not Present) as an alternative and improved approach, given the specific features of the commercial credit card market. ASB Issuing notes variance between the blended average interchange rate on commercial credit cards when specifically isolating Card Present and Card Not Present transactions. Moreover, an ever-increasing share of commercial credit card transactions are Card Not Present today. For ASB, of commercial credit card transactions occur online (Card Not Present), whilst ASB's consumer credit card book sees A level of approximately 1.1% for all market participants is simple for merchants and acquirers to understand, sustains some degree of domestic issuer value and still delivers a top line saving for New Zealand businesses. ASB notes commercial credit card transactions occur at merchants receiving preferential 'Strategic Merchant' and 'Industry Program' interchange rates, meaning the ASB does not believe there should be a distinct or higher interchange cap for foreign issued cards. Rather a net neutral position should be found which simplifies implementation and evens the playing field.

Commerce Commission 'targeted question' 3.2: The relevance and scale of the incremental issuer costs (fraud losses, fraud prevention measures, and administrative costs).

Cost should be considered in the context of revenue, as larger/heighted costs can be more/less offset depending on the per card income being generated. Simply focusing on the cost differential between consumer and commercial card portfolios reflects only a component of the value exchange. As mentioned in 2.1, the revolve rate on a commercial credit card meaning fewer customers pay interest. As fewer customers pay interest on a commercial credit card than a consumer product, offsetting any lost interchange income via fees and/or interest income would require a disproportionately high degree of cross subsidisation. The costs associated with providing a commercial credit card product are far from limited to fraud. In the 12-months to May 2026, ASB recorded fraud rate rather than the 1 — 2 bps quoted in the consultation 6

7 paper. In addition to fraud, ASB carries a higher degree of credit risk (which is not born by the merchant accepting the payment) on commercial cards vs consumer.

Furthermore, commercial credit cards provide many businesses critical working capital – essentially extending interest-free liquidity to business and providing float. Interchange is the primary mechanism to sustain the issuer-to-cardholder value exchange. The product provides liquidity to business and facilitates a safer and most cost-effective means to transact. However, providing cardholders access to these funds, costs ASB considerably. The Cost of Funds associated with enabling a credit card portfolio is often erroneously considered applicable to revolvers only – those customers carrying a balance and paying interest. This is not the case. Issuers make merchants whole upfront and are repaid by the cardholders in the future. The cost associated with providing these funds Clearly, there are a myriad of direct and allocated costs associated with maintaining a credit card portfolio that would provide little value to state line by line. Rather, ASB would ask the Commission to consider a more rounded (total) cost to income approach to evaluate sustainability rather than anchoring the analysis purely to cost differentials between consumer and commercial cards.
Commerce Commission ‘targeted question’ 4.1: Whether the proposed interchange caps may affect your ability and/or incentives to enter, expand, or compete in commercial credit card issuance; and Commerce Commission ‘targeted question’ 4.2 Potential impacts on pricing, product viability, or investment decisions
The proposed commercial credit card caps may prompt issuers to consider revenue levers within their portfolios and/or cost savings, and may impact issuers’ future commercial credit card investment.
Unlike consumer credit cards, commercial cards are not a requisite product for a credit card issuer.
Commerce Commission ‘targeted question’ 6. We propose that the limiting mechanism apply to domestic commercial credit transactions only, on the basis that foreign-issued transactions do not appear to have preferential interchange categories. Do any preferential, negotiated, or industry specific interchange rates exist for foreign-issued commercial credit card transactions? If so, please briefly describe their structure and use in practice.

We suggest Visa and Mastercard are best placed to answer this question Commerce Commission ‘targeted question’ 8 & 9: We are interested in card acquirer views on whether a proposed two to three-month implementation period would be sufficient to implement the commercial credit card interchange fee caps and associated limiting mechanism. For example, we welcome views on: • The adequacy of this timeframe based on your experience. • Implementing interchange fee changes following the Commission’s July 2025 decision, including repricing activity.
The proposed two- to three-month implementation period is materially insufficient to make the required system changes. A compressed timeframe increases the risk of incorrect merchant billing, which could result in merchant customers being overcharged and requiring subsequent remediation.
After the Commission confirms the caps, the card schemes must confirm the applicable rates, criteria and effective date for interchange rate changes being made to comply with the caps. Until these details have been received, which is generally through a scheme bulletin, our ability to understand the extent of the changes or the implementation effort and timeframes is not known.

Any interchange rate changes would require ASB to:

(a)make material changes to the third-party merchant system implementation of the new rates;

(b)update pricing models financial monitoring, reporting and controls, with some activities dependent o
(c)redesign controls to monitor compliance with the Retail Payments System Act; and (d)communicate the changes to affected customers.

As a result, we recommend that the Commission consider the dependency on the card schemes confirming the interchange rate changes in determining the implementation timeframe for the commercial credit card interchange fee caps.

Our preference would be for the Commission to set an implementation date of no earlier than 8 months from the announcement date and for the Commission to direct the schemes to confirm applicable rates and criteria no later than 2 months from announcement date to allow acquirers 6 months for implementation.

Bank of New Zealand's response to Commerce Commission's Commercial Credit Card Interchange Fee Regulation for Mastercard and Visa networks - Draft Decision and Reasons paper.

1 Introduction
1.1 BNZ welcomes the opportunity to respond to the Commerce Commission’s consultation on the Commercial Credit Card Interchange Fee Regulation for Mastercard and Visa networks – Draft Decision and Reasons paper. We support the Commission’s objective of promoting competition, transparency and fairer outcomes across the retail payments system.

1.2 BNZ notes that commercial credit cards are not simply consumer credit cards for business. They carry different fraud, servicing and administrative considerations. BNZ is broadly supportive of the introduction of caps on commercial cards, although in our view, the currently proposed level is too low and is not reflective of the specific characteristics of commercial cards.

1.3 Our submission is focused on three points:

1.3.1 Inclusion of other payment networks: the Commission should monitor for shifts to less-regulated payment schemes and consider regulating other payment systems where this results in higher merchant costs or acceptance pressure.
1.3.2 Simplicity for merchants: we recommend a minimum six‑month implementation period between the final decision and commencement to support effective implementation.
1.3.3 Consequences of lower interchange revenue: the Commission should clearly assess the downstream consequences of lower interchange revenue, including fraud, product sustainability, innovation and investment impacts.

2 Inclusion of other payment networks
2.1 BNZ encourages the Commission to consider the position of comparable payment networks as part of the final decision. We note that the draft decision recognises the risk that commercial cardholders may switch to alternative payment networks, to retain rewards and other benefits.

2.2 BNZ considers this a critical point. We believe that if the Commission reduces interchange revenue on Mastercard and Visa commercial credit cards, but leaves comparable schemes outside the regulatory perimeter, there is a clear risk that spend continues the shift towards less regulated or higher-cost payment systems. We believe that outcome may not necessarily reduce merchant costs overall, but instead may simply move, and even increase, costs from one part of the payment system to another.
2.3 We encourage the Commission to set out a clear approach to monitoring and, designate other payment systems and issue a comparable network standard where there is evidence of substitution, rising merchant costs, or increased acceptance pressure. We believe the goal should be a neutral framework that supports lower costs and better transparency across the payments ecosystem, not just within the Mastercard and Visa networks.

3 Simplicity for merchants
3.1 BNZ strongly supports simplicity and transparency in the retail payments system. This has been a consistent theme in our previous submission. We acknowledge the Commission's efforts to simply the interchange framework for Mastercard and Visa transactions. The proposal for commercial credit cards, if set at a sustainable level, together with aligning pre-paid card interchange settings, would substantially complete the remaining part of the interchange framework for those schemes.

3.2 Regarding implementation, we note the staged nature of the interchange reforms to date has required merchants and market participants to respond to multiple rounds of change, including pricing and communication impacts. BNZ supports timely implementation that is clean, clear, and consistent. The new proposed changes are likely to require scheme co-ordination, system updates, further pricing changes, governance, and communication to merchant customers. We strongly encourage the Commission to ensure the implementation period provides enough certainty for participants to apply the final settings properly and avoid unnecessary operational risk or inconsistent market outcomes.

3.3 To illustrate this, we refer to the greater merchant choice enabled by BNZ offering different merchant service fee pricing options. BNZ offers both 'interchange plus' and alternative pricing options. There is a considerable modelling effort to implement new regulatory caps for all merchants on alternative pricing. This usually involves around a six-month process from the point rates are published through to implementation. We also note that this may be the third recent repricing for some merchants, following repricing in December 2025 and May 2026. Therefore, BNZ strongly recommends a period of at least six months from the final decision date before the new settings take effect.

3.4 We also urge the Commission to avoid a pre-Christmas deployment to ensure participants can implement the changes in a controlled manner and not during the year's busiest trading period.

3.5 We believe the commencement date in clause 2 of the network standard should be amended to identify when the commercial credit card caps come into effect. The 2

3 present drafting of the draft Amendment Network Standard does not include placeholder drafting to insert a date for when the new caps would come into effect.
4 Consequences of lower interchange revenue
4.1 BNZ recognises that the Commission is seeking to reduce merchant service fees and improve outcomes for merchants and consumers. However, we believe that lower interchange revenue is not without consequence. We strongly suggest that the final decision paper clearly considers the downstream effects of materially reducing interchange revenue for commercial credit card products.

4.2 We note that these products involve different customer needs, use cases, risk considerations, and serving requirements from consumer credit cards. BNZ is concerned that the proposed caps are so low that they may affect issuer incentives to maintain and invest in commercial card functionality, fraud controls, and customer support.
4.3 BNZ considers the final decision would benefit from more directly addressing these potential consequences:

4.3.1 Whether lower interchange revenue could affect investment in fraud prevention and security controls;

4.3.2 Whether commercial card product features, reporting, controls, or servicing could be affected over time;

4.3.3 Whether costs may shift to business cardholders through fees;

4.3.4 Whether issuers will retain sufficient incentive to invest in commercial card functionality;

4.3.5 Whether substitution to other payment systems could reduce or offset expected merchant benefits; and
4.3.6 Whether small businesses that rely on commercial cards for business purchasing could face reduced functionality or increased direct costs.

5 Conclusion
5.1 BNZ supports the Commerce Commission’s objective of promoting a more efficient, transparent and competitive payments system. We are broadly supportive of appropriate commercial credit card interchange caps and encourage the Commission to ensure a consistent regulatory framework across comparable payment networks, allow sufficient time for implementation, and give careful consideration to the broader impacts of reduced interchange revenue on innovation, product availability and sustainability, and business customers. We appreciate the opportunity to provide feedback and look forward to continuing to engage with the Commission on the Retail Payment System Act 2022.

5.2 Should the Commerce Commission have any questions in relation to this submission, please contact Paul Hay on the details below:

Yours sincerely

4
Paul Hay
Āpiha Matua: Waeture me te Tūtohu (Chief Regulatory and Compliance Officer) Bank of New Zealand

Citibank NZ Submission on Commercial Card Interchange Regulation

Submission to the
Commerce Commission of New Zealand by
Citibank, N.A. New Zealand on
Retail Payment System
Commercial Credit Card Interchange Fee
Regulation for Mastercard and Visa Networks
- Draft Decision and Reasons Paper
17th July 2026

Page 2
Background
Citibank NA - New Zealand (“Citi”) welcomes the opportunity to provide feedback on the Commerce Commission’s Draft Decision and Reasons Paper in relation to Commercial Credit Card Interchange Fee Regulation (“Draft Decision”).

Citi is a global financial institution with an established presence in New Zealand, providing financial services, including the issuance of Commercial Card products to wholesale clients including public sector clients. We support Commercial Card programmes across multiple jurisdictions and therefore bring experience from a range of market structures and operating environments.

Citi believes that it is important that any regulatory approach reflects both the distinct economics of Commercial Cards and the structural characteristics of the New Zealand market.

Commercial Cards are fundamentally different from consumer credit cards, both in their design and underlying economics. They are typically structured as charge card products, used for higher-value and predictable business expenditures, where balances are settled in full each billing cycle. As such:

-  Commercial Cards do not generate any material interest income

-  Interchange fees represent the primary revenue stream supporting these products

-  Commercial Cards provide integrated payment, data, reporting capabilities, and business process automation efficiencies.

Commercial Cards deliver significant value to corporates, public sector entities, and merchants by:

-  Streamlining accounts payable and expense management processes

-  Providing enhanced transaction data and reporting flows, often integrated directly into Enterprise Resource Planning (“ERP”) systems

-  Supporting working capital optimisation

-  Reducing administrative burden and operational cost, enabling them to re-deploy resources to tasks with higher added value.

These capabilities require ongoing investment in technology, servicing, and integration.
They are a key differentiator compared to consumer credit card offerings.
Executive Summary
We support the Commission’s objective of improving outcomes in the payments ecosystem. However, we are concerned that the Draft Decision, in its current form, may lead to unintended consequences for competition, innovation, and business efficiency.
New Zealand is a smaller and more concentrated market relative to comparator jurisdictions such as Australia. According to World Bank data, New Zealand’s population is approximately 5.3 million compared to approximately 27.2 million in Australia, while New Zealand’s GDP is approximately USD 260 billion compared to approximately USD 1.76 trillion in Australia.

Page 3
This difference in scale is reflected in a narrower issuer base and lower transaction volumes, reducing the market’s ability to absorb regulatory changes without impacting participation and investment.

Against this backdrop, we consider that the Draft Decision:

-  Does not fully reflect the economics of Commercial Cards, which are not revolving products and rely on interchange fees as their primary revenue source.

-  Proposal of a 20 basis point premium to consumer interchange is insufficient, and does not adequately reflect the cost and value structure of Commercial Card programmes.

-  Does not include complementary measures (e.g. surcharging or acquiring transparency), creating uncertainty as to whether the benefits will be passed through.

-  May alter competitive dynamics, including creating relative advantages for threeparty network providers and increasing the risk of structural market shifts over time.

-  May diminish the commercial viability of investment in product innovation, fraud controls, tokenisation and digital wallet capabilities, given the smaller New Zealand market where programme scale is limited.

-  May disproportionately affect SMEs, through lower availability of credit, as issuers tighten credit criteria. This may lead to increased costs to merchants, as buyers request extended payment terms, to offset reduction in working capital benefits provided by Commercial Cards. Additional costs include bad debt collection, manual processing costs, and cash collection costs. Korefusion Research
1 states that Merchants can significantly reduce bad debts, by 106 basis points on average, thanks to a guarantee provided by card acceptance.

-  In a market with a more concentrated issuer base, even modest changes in participation or investment may have a disproportionate impact on competition, choice, and innovation.

Taken together, we consider that the Draft Decision may risk creating a less competitive and less innovative payments ecosystem and may place New Zealand corporates at a relative disadvantage compared to global peers.

Response to Targeted Questions
Question 2. Issuer’s view on Commerce Commission approach at steps 1 and 2 of their methodology:

How does the New Zealand market differ from comparator markets?

Citi notes the Commerce Commission has used the Australian interchange fee as a benchmark for New Zealand interchange. However, Australia's Commercial Card market benefits from materially higher transaction volumes, larger corporate programmes, and a
1 Korefusion “From Transaction to Transformation Quantifying the Value of B2B Card Acceptance to Large Asia Pacific Suppliers” June 2025 P13 commissioned by Visa

Page 4
greater ability to spread fixed technology, compliance and servicing costs. These structural differences reduce the direct comparability of the Australian interchange fee settings.
The Commission's reliance on Australian outcomes therefore risks overstating the ability of the New Zealand market to absorb similar reductions.

Unlike Australia, New Zealand does not include regulation of surcharging or acquiring practices, meaning there is no certainty that interchange reductions will be passed through.
Citi’s view is that this is a material difference between Australia and New Zealand. The result may simply be a redistribution of revenue within the payments value chain rather than a reduction in merchant acceptance costs. Any regulation of Commercial Card interchange fees, should be accompanied by regulatory intervention with regards to acquiring and surcharging fees.

Issuer’s feedback on the additional 10 basis point premium above domestic personal credit card interchange to promote stable and sustainable market outcomes and mitigate the risk of market disruption.

Citi does not view the 10 basis point premium as sufficient to reflect the differences in the products or to prevent market disruption. Given the size of the New Zealand market, the premium may be insufficient to attract issuers to the market and support ongoing investment in innovative products in New Zealand.

Question 3. Issuer’s view on Commerce Commission’s approach at step 3 of their methodology:

We do not consider the proposed 10 basis points premium to domestic personal credit card interchange to be sufficient for Commercial Cards for the following reasons:

-  The proposal does not reflect the absence of interest income on Commercial Cards provided to medium to large corporate and public sector clients; interchange is the primary source of revenue.

-  The proposal does not adequately account for higher servicing and capability requirements for Commercial Card products which require set up of client-specific configurations, controls, approval chains and data file feeds.

-  Commercial Card programmes provide a unique two-sided working capital benefit to both buyers and suppliers through the use of the issuer's balance sheet. Buyers receive extended payment terms while merchants typically receive payment within one to two days of transaction settlement. The extended payment terms Commercial Cards provide buyers, provides suppliers with the opportunity to modify payment terms when they accept cards. KoreFusion research paper estimates the potential benefit at approximately 15 days of accelerated payment2.
This creates a valuable source of liquidity for both parties and reduces the need for alternative financing arrangements. Regulatory intervention that reduces the economics supporting these programmes may weaken benefits for participants.
2 Korefusion “From Transaction to Transformation Quantifying the Value of B2B Card Acceptance to Large Asia Pacific Supplier” June 2025 P17 Commissioned by Visa

Page 5

-  Citi is of the view that the cost of funding interest free periods should be considered in the interchange rate, given the sharing of the working capital benefits to the merchant. A material change in interchange will reduce the ability of issuers to continue providing these working capital benefits under current terms, potentially leading to shorter payment cycles, reduced credit availability and increased financing costs for New Zealand businesses.

Difference in approach for In-person and Online card use Citi notes that implementing two distinct interchange rates for in-person and online is a meaningful variation to the Reserve Bank of Australia’s approach. This will provide significant pricing complexity to issuers as each client will have different in-person and online spend patterns. This differentiation, coupled with the limiting mechanism, could be significantly lower that then assumed 78bps implied weighted average in New Zealand.
Question 4. Issuer’s views on the impacts on competition The Draft Decision may have adverse impacts on competition for the following reasons:
Three-party payment network advantage

-  Three-party payment networks are not subject to the same constraints, yet they target the same flows and proposition. As such they will gain a relative competitive advantage, particularly in the commercial segment, accelerating migration towards the three-party payment networks.

-  While American Express may currently represent a relatively small share of the overall New Zealand payments market, its position within commercial payments is growing. The Draft Decision will create an uneven playing field.

-  The three-party payment network advantage may ultimately create the need for future regulatory intervention to address distortions in competitive outcomes that could be avoided through more proportionate settings today.

Issuer participation

-  Existing issuers may scale back their offerings or potentially exit the market given the significantly lower interchange fees they would be able to charge in New Zealand.

-  The Draft Decision may result in the only issuers remaining in New Zealand being those who are able to cross subsidise across other aspects of their business.
Innovation and digital competition
Investment by issuers of Commercial Cards into innovative products such as digital wallets, tokenisation and virtual cards integration into wallets, could be constrained given the lower revenue streams issuers will face.

Page 6
Commercial Card propositions require ongoing investment in fraud prevention, cyber resilience, authentication capabilities and transaction monitoring to address increasingly sophisticated financial crime threats.

These capabilities are increasingly central to modern corporate payment solutions, and reduced investment may limit innovation, adoption and security for New Zealand corporates compared to their international peers.

Question 9. Issuer’s view on whether the proposed two-to-three month implementation period is sufficient to implement required pricing and system changes.

A two-to-three month implementation period would not be achievable for many issuers and corporate clients.

Any material restructuring of programme economics may require changes to client contractual agreements, systems, reporting processes and accounting workflows which could take multiple system releases to fully encapsulate, in the world of corporate technology development.

For this reason, Citi recommends a minimum implementation period of twelve months, mapped to four major quarterly technology releases if the Draft Decision is implemented.
Summary of Citi’s Recommendations:

-  Continue to exclude Commercial Card interchange from regulatory cap, or if the Commerce Commission decides to proceed with implementing a cap then implement a materially higher Commercial Card interchange differential than that proposed in the Draft Decision

-  Introduce complementary measures addressing acquiring transparency and surcharging before implementing interchange reductions

-  Reconsider the proposed differentiation between in person and online Commercial Card interchange rates

-  Allow a minimum of 12 months implementation period Conclusion
While the objectives of the proposal are acknowledged, we consider that, in its current form, it may:

-  Misalign with Commercial Card economics

-  Reduce competition and innovation

-  Fail to ensure achieved benefits are passed through

-  Lead to product simplification or reduced availability

-  Lead to a reduction in fraud protection and its future enhancements

-  Create a long-term disadvantage for competitiveness of New Zealand businesses

Page 7
Changes to interchange settings may alter competitive dynamics, including by increasing the relative attractiveness of three-party payments network. This creates a risk of structural shifts in the market that should instead be addressed at the outset.
We therefore encourage the Commission to adopt an approach that reflects the distinct characteristics of Commercial Cards and supports a competitive, innovative, and efficient commercial payments ecosystem.

1 13 July 2026 Payments Team
Commerce Commission
Wellington By email: PaymentsTeam@comcom.govt.nz Commercial Credit Interchange
Thank you for the opportunity for the Financial Services Federation (the FSF) to submit on the Consultation document on the Commercial Credit Interchange discussion paper (the Consultation).

By way of background, the FSF is the industry body representing specialist lenders operating in New Zealand. We have around 100 members (a list of which is attached as Appendix A) which include motor vehicle finance providers, fleet leasing providers, specialist housing lenders, Deposit Takers, the larger finance companies operating in New Zealand, commercial asset leasing and finance providers, credit-related insurers and Affiliate members which include internationally recognised legal and consulting partners.
Our members provide their products and services to more than 1.5 million New Zealand consumers and businesses. Data relating to the extent to which FSF members (excluding Affiliate members) contribute to New Zealand consumers, society and the economy is attached as Appendix B.
We would like to make the comment that we are accepting of the direction that interchange fees are heading in due to the fact this has been signalled. Despite this we are concerned at the impact this will have on competition as we have previously highlighted in our submissions. You will note that one of our members went a long way towards developing a commercial credit card offering but pulled out due to the signalled interchange changes.
This is a direct negative impact on competition from the work the Commission is doing. We believe more thought should be put into the unintended consequences on competition that the Commissions payment work is creating.
Please do not hesitate to contact me if you have any queries.
Yours sincerely,
Katie Rawlinson
Legal and Policy Manager
Financial Services Federation

May 21, 2026 3
1.0 Executive summary
1. This submission has been prepared by Keystone on behalf of Mastercard to support engagement with the New Zealand Commerce Commission (NZCC) in the context of its review of the market for commercial cards in New Zealand. It provides an economic assessment grounded in publicly available evidence, Mastercard internal information, and established principles of platform economics.

2. Keystone (www.Keystone.com) is an economic and strategy consulting firm with offices in the US and London, which focuses on competition matters in tech markets. This report has been authored by Dr Andrea Coscelli, Senior Partner and former Chief Executive Officer of the UK Competition and Markets Authority, Dr George Charlson, Senior Engagement Manager, Francesca Montrasio, Engagement Manager and Paripoorna Baxi, Consultant.

3. Commercial cards are an important component of the B2B payments ecosystem.1 They are used by businesses and public sector entities across a wide range of purchasing contexts, including procurement, supplier payments, travel and entertainment, and online transactions. Commercial cards operate within organised card networks2that coordinate multiple participants whose decisions are interdependent. Four-party card networks (such as Mastercard or Visa) are comprised of cardholders, merchants, issuers and acquirers.

3
In the case of commercial cards, the “cardholder” is the buyer business while the supplier business is the card-accepting merchant.
In four-party networks,
4 the interchange fee reallocates value between the issuing and acquiring sides of the platform and is a component of the merchant service fee (MSF).

5
1 We define commercial cards as business or corporate debit, credit or prepaid cards that are issued to businesses or their employees for business expenses. See European Parliament & Council, “Regulation (EU) 2015/751 of the European Parliament and of the Council of 29 April 2015 on interchange fees for card-based payment transactions,” (19 May 2015), available at https://eur-lex.europa.eu/eli/reg/2015/751/oj/eng.
2 Card networks refer to the card payment organisations that regulate and control activity on the payment network, such as Visa and Mastercard. This is the term used by the Reserve Bank of Australia (“RBA”) and NZCC. Instead, the European Commission and Payment Systems Regulator use the term card scheme. We stick with card network following the terminology used by the RBA/NZCC. See Reserve Bank of Australia, “Review of Merchant Card Payment Costs and Surcharging Conclusions Paper,” p.4 (31 March 2026), available at https://www.rba.gov.au/payments-and-infrastructure/review-of-retail-payments-regulation/2026- 03/conclusions-paper/pdf/conclusions-paper.pdf.

3 We define each of these actors as follows: (i) cardholders are the buyer businesses that use commercial cards to make payments; (ii) merchants are the supplier businesses that accept commercial cards as a payment instrument; (iii) issuers are typically banks that provide cards to commercial cardholders; (iv) acquirers are typically banks that provide merchants with the infrastructure to accept card payments and process transactions. Please refer to Section 2.1 for a full set of terminology and definitions.
4 A four-party card network (four-party network) is a network where card-based transactions are made through the intermediation of the card network, an issuer, and an acquirer (for example, Mastercard and Visa). In contrast, a three-party card network (three-party network) is one in which the network itself provides acquiring and issuing services (for example, Amex). See EU Interchange Fee Regulation (2015).
5 When a commercial card transaction occurs in a four-party network, the issuer transfers the transaction value to the acquirer, minus an interchange fee. The interchange fee is the transaction-based fee paid by the acquirer to the issuer in a card-based payment transaction and the merchant service charge (MSC) (or merchant

May 21, 2026 4
4. Because commercial card networks are two-sided platforms,6the interchange fee cannot be assessed solely as a merchant-facing cost. It reallocates value across both sides of the platform and influences participation, usage, pricing, credit provision, service quality, and investment decisions on both the issuing and acquiring sides. Assessing the appropriate level of interchange therefore depends not only on the level of fees, but on how value is allocated to sustain usage and investment across all participants.

5. The report sets out the main economic and operational benefits generated by commercial cards for both cardholders and merchants. These include liquidity and working-capital support, operational efficiency, enhanced payment security, and revenue and growth effects for merchants. These benefits are central to understanding the value created by commercial card systems and the role of interchange fees in supporting that value.

6. In practice, commercial cards support a range of economic and operational functions that extend beyond simple payment execution. They combine payment functionality with short-term financing and operational tools. Businesses use them to manage cash flow and access liquidity at the point of transaction, while merchants benefit from faster and more predictable settlement, reduced collection risk, and streamlined reconciliation processes.7 The integration of payment, credit, and data can reduce administrative costs and support more efficient business operations for both cardholders and merchants. These benefits are relevant when assessing how pricing structures influence participation and investment within the ecosystem.

7. The New Zealand context further shapes the relevance of commercial cards. The New Zealand economy is characterised by a high prevalence of small businesses, widespread use of trade credit, and exposure to payment delays in B2B transactions amongst New Zealand businesses.
As a small open economy, New Zealand is also vulnerable to external, international shocks that can affect domestic firm-level cash flow and financing conditions,
8 which increases the importance of flexible payment and working capital tools. In such an environment, tools that support liquidity, payment certainty, and short-term financial flexibility are particularly important for business resilience.

6 Two-sided platforms refer to intermediaries that bring together two distinct groups of users and enable interactions between them, where the value of participation on one side depends on participation on the other.
See Rochet & Tirole, “Two-Sided Markets: A Progress Report,” (2006), available at https://www.jstor.org/stable/25046265.

7 Reconciliation is the process of matching and verifying transaction records against entries in bank statements or payment system records to ensure that all funds moving into and out of an account are accurately recorded and consistent. Global economic uncertainty, and in particular trade policy uncertainty, increased significantly during the first half of 2025.”).

May 21, 2026 5
8. Commercial cards also differ materially from consumer cards.9 They are more heavily creditbased, involve larger credit lines and higher spend per-account, and are typically embedded within broader banking and commercial relationships. They are also associated with reconciliation and account-management features,
10 enhanced controls like spend limits and restrictions on types of purchases,
11 and integration with business systems. These features mean that commercial cards serve distinct economic functions and require a separate analytical framework from consumer card products.

9. Publicly available evidence suggests that commercial cards operate in New Zealand within a competitive and evolving B2B payments ecosystem alongside bank transfers, three-party networks, and emerging fintech and open-banking solutions. In this context, interchange fees play a role in sustaining participation and coordinating incentives across the platform, and changes to it may have effects beyond any immediate change in merchant charges, including impacts on credit provision, usage patterns, and substitution across payment instruments. Importantly, flexible, unregulated interchange ensures competitive neutrality is maintained between four-party networks and three-party networks.

10. The final section of this submission develops an economic framework for assessing how interchange fees sustain participation and investment in commercial card markets. It also explains why, in a two-sided system, capping interchange fees is likely to have system-wide effects (including altered issuer incentives, changes in cardholder usage, and potential shifts by merchants toward alternative payment instruments) rather than simply delivering straightforward reductions in merchant costs. It then reviews international best practice and shows that the most relevant overseas comparators support maintaining greater flexibility for commercial card interchange fees than for consumer cards.

2.0 Overview of commercial card networks and their role in sustaining the B2B payment ecosystem
11. This section provides an overview of how commercial card networks operate within multi-party B2B payment systems, the role played by interchange fees in sustaining participation across these systems, and the broader economic context in which these arrangements function. It first explains the structure of commercial card networks, including the roles of issuers, acquirers, cardholders (buyer businesses) and merchants (supplier businesses), and how interchange fees contribute to balancing incentives across these interdependent participants (Section 2.1). It then sets out the economic and operational benefits that commercial cards deliver to both cardholders
9 Consumer cards are card-based payment instruments issued to a consumer, defined as a natural person who is acting for purposes other than the trade, business or profession of that person. See EU Interchange Fee Regulation (2015).

10 Capgemini, “The Future of Commercial Cards,” (2015), available at https://www.capgemini.com/mx-es/wpcontent/uploads/sites/28/2022/12/the_future_of_commercial_cards.pdf (“Financial institutions can provide program management tools to help manage various aspects of a commercial cards program such as reporting, workflow management, dashboards, reconciliation, and account management.”).

For example, businesses can set merchant category codes (MCC) to limit where the cards may be used.”).

May 21, 2026 6 and merchants, including in areas such as liquidity,12 working capital management, operational efficiency, and revenue generation (Section 2.2). Together, these sections provide the foundation for understanding how commercial card networks should be analysed in the New Zealand context from an economics lens.

2.1 Commercial card networks operate within multi-party networks in which interchange sustains participation
12. Commercial cards are payment cards issued to businesses and public sector entities to facilitate payments for goods and services. They are used in a range of contexts, including point-of-sale purchases, online procurement, travel and entertainment expenses, and supplier payments.
13. Like consumer cards, commercial cards operate within organised card payment networks.13 Card networks (such as Visa, Mastercard, and American Express) provide the infrastructure, operating rules, and pricing frameworks that allow the system to function. There are two primary types of card networks: four-party networks and three-party networks.

14. In a four-party commercial card network (such as Visa or Mastercard), four distinct entities participate in each transaction (in addition to the network operator itself): cardholders, merchants, issuers and acquirers.

a. Cardholders are the buyer businesses that use commercial cards to make payments.14 Commercial cardholders are businesses ranging from sole proprietors and small and medium-sized enterprises to large corporate and public sector entities. They benefit from payment functionality, short-term credit (where applicable), security protections, and operational tools such as expense tracking and reporting.

b. Merchants are the supplier businesses that accept commercial cards as a payment instrument.

15 Merchants benefit from card acceptance16 through faster settlement,17 lower
12 Liquidity refers to the ability of an economic agent to meet their short-term financial obligations. See OECD, “OECD Glossary of Statistical Terms,” p.309 (1 September 2008), available at https://www.oecd.org/en/publications/oecd-glossary-of-statistical-terms_9789264055087-en.html.
13 We refer to a payment network as the infrastructure over which payments take place, and a card network as the card payment organisation that regulates and controls activity on the payment network.

Payment instruments are the means by which a payment is initiated (e.g. cards, bank transfers).

16 Card acceptance refers to a merchant’s ability to accept and process payment card transactions through an acquiring bank or service provider. See European Central Bank, “All glossary entries,” available at https://www.ecb.europa.eu/services/glossary/html/glossa.en.html.

17 Settlement is defined as the process by which a card network facilitates the exchange of funds between the issuer and the acquirer, thereby completing the transaction and enabling the funds to be made available to the merchant. See Stripe, “Payment settlement explained: How it works and how long it takes,” (14 October 2025), available at https://stripe.com/gb/resources/more/payment-settlement-explained-how-it-works-and-how-long-ittakes.

May 21, 2026 7 reconciliation costs,18 reduced cash handling, improved payment security, and in some cases guaranteed payment relative to invoicing arrangements.

c. Issuers are typically banks that provide commercial cards to commercial cardholders. They extend credit, set credit limits,19 and bear the costs of issuing (cardholder acquisition, customer service, card features and rewards, processing, cost of funds, credit losses, and fraud losses).

d. Acquirers are typically banks that provide merchants with the infrastructure to accept card payments and process transactions.21
15. When a commercial card transaction occurs in a four-party network, the issuer transfers the transaction value to the acquirer, minus an interchange fee. The interchange fee is a transactionbased fee paid by the acquirer to the issuer. The acquirer, in turn, charges the merchant an MSF, 22 for card acceptance and processing services. The interchange fee is an important component of the MSF, among scheme and processing fees,23 and acquirer margins.24 The card network does not receive any interchange revenue.

16. Commercial card transactions may occur in both point-of-sale (POS) and invoiced payment contexts. In POS transactions, the value proposition of commercial cards is often linked to access to credit, faster funding, and product functionality such as spending controls and fraud protection.
18 Visa/KoreFusion, “From Transaction to Transformation: Quantifying the Value of B2B Card Acceptance to Large Asia Pacific Suppliers,” p.19 (June 2025), available at https://corporate.visa.com/en/solutions/commercial-solutions/knowledge-hub/total-value-of-commercial-creditcard-acceptance.html (“For example, reconciliation problems are reported by 78% of merchants and affect 1.79% of invoices at a cost of 1.7% of invoice value. Among card acceptors, 53% report benefits in this area, achieving a 63% reduction in reconciliation issues.”).

19 Credit limits refer to the maximum amount that a borrower is permitted to draw under a credit facility at a particular time. 22 NZCC, “Retail Payment System: Costs to businesses and consumers of card payments in Aotearoa New Zealand: Consultation Paper,” p.12 (23 July 2024), available at https://www.comcom.govt.nz/__data/assets/pdf_file/0031/359491/Retail-Payment-System-Costs-tobusinesses-and-consumers-of-card-payments-in-Aotearoa-New-Zealand-Consultation-Paper-23-July2024.pdf?trk=public_post_comment-text (“This paper is mainly focused on interchange fees [...] These fees form a significant portion of the merchant service fee”);

23 Scheme and processing fees are the fees charged by card networks to acquirers and issuers for the provision of card payment services. Scheme fees relate to participation in the scheme, while processing fees are charged for the authorisation, clearing, and settlement of payments. In invoiced payment contexts, transactions typically occur within an existing commercial relationship where counterparties directly agree on payment terms.

17. Card networks define the default interchange fee unless the issuer and acquirer have agreed upon a different bilateral interchange fee. While the interchange fee is paid between banks (issuers & acquirers), its economic incidence is ultimately shared between merchants and cardholders, depending on competitive conditions and the extent of pass-through.25 Figure 1: Flow of fees in a four-party network26
18. Since the four-party networks involve multiple interdependent participants, interchange fees affect participation incentives across the ecosystem, reallocating value from the acquiring side of the platform to the issuing side and serving to balance incentives between card issuance and merchant acceptance.27
25 Shabgard & Asensio, “The price effects of reducing payment card interchange fees,” p.190 (27 April 2023), available at https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s13209-023-00278-y (“A higher IF results in a higher cost of accepting card payments for the merchant, which he may pass through to consumers in the form of higher retail prices.”).

Pass-through is defined as the proportion of a change in interchange fees that is reflected in the merchant service charges (or merchant service fees) paid by merchants to acquirers. The determinants of pass-through include the pricing model (e.g. whether the plan is blended or unblended), the merchant's bargaining position, and the level of competition between acquirers. See Payment Systems Regulator, “Market review into the supply of card-acquiring services: Final report,” p.66 (November 2021), available at https://www.psr.org.uk/media/p1tlg0iw/psr-card-acquiring-market-review-final-report-november-2021.pdf.
26 Direction of arrow represents flow of fees (e.g., the merchant pays the MSC [or MSF] to the acquiring bank etc.). Dotted line represents a contractual relationship.

27 Börestam & Schmiedel, “Interchange fees in card payments,” p.11 (September 2011), available at https://www.ecb.europa.eu/pub/pdf/scpops/ecbocp131.pdf (“The interchange fee [...] is paid by the acquirer to the issuer. In other words, interchange fees are basically a balancing mechanism through which some of the costs on the issuing side are covered by the acquiring side.”).

May 21, 2026 9
a. Issuers. Issuers generate revenue from interest income, annual or programme fees, and receive interchange fees from acquirers.

28 Interchange fees play a key role in covering the costs associated with credit risk,29 capital allocation,30 fraud management, and account servicing.31 If interchanges fees are reduced and issuer revenue decreases, issuers may respond by increasing cardholder fees, reducing benefits and innovation in new features, lowering credit limits, and/or adjusting rewards structures.

32
b. Cardholders. The level of interchange fees also impacts the value proposition available to cardholders. Issuer revenue, including from interchange fees, helps fund the costs of providing value-added services and rewards to cardholders. A lower level of interchange can impact the provision of these services and affect the attractiveness of commercial cards and change cardholder usage patterns.

c. Acquirers. Acquirers pay interchange fees to issuers and recover this cost through the MSF.

33 The interchange fee is a per-transaction transfer (typically calculated as a percentage of transaction value) that the acquirer passes through to the issuer, rather than a retained cost in the conventional sense. As such, a higher interchange fee increases the portion of each transaction value that is passed on to the issuer, reducing the net amount retained by the acquirer from that transaction (all else equal). Acquirers therefore have an incentive to pass this additional cost on to merchants through the MSF, but the extent to which they can do so depends on competitive conditions.

34
28 Additionally, issuers may generate revenue from other sources such as late payment fees and cross-border exchange rate gains. Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System, “Credit Card Profitability,” (9 September 2022), available at https://www.federalreserve.gov/econres/notes/feds-notes/credit-cardprofitability-20220909.html (“The last source of credit card revenue is usage fees, including late fees, over-limit fees, foreign exchange fees, and so on.”)

29 Credit risk refers to the risk that a borrower will fail to meet its contractual financial obligations in full and on time, causing the lender to incur financial loss. See OECD Glossary of Statistical Terms, p.111 (2008).
30 Capital allocation refers to the process by which a bank assigns its equity capital to different activities to support operations, meet regulatory requirements, and manage performance. See Bank of England, “Banks’ internal capital markets: how do banks allocate capital internally?,” p.3 (2018), available at https://www.bankofengland.co.uk/-/media/boe/files/quarterly-bulletin/2018/banks-internal-capital-markets-howdo-banks-allocate-capital-internally.
31 Stripe, “Interchange fees – the basics: What they are, how they work and how to cut costs,” (12 March 2024), available at https://stripe.com/gb/resources/more/interchange-fees-101-what-they-are-how-they-work-and-howto-cut-costs (“Interchange fees serve several purposes [...] compensate the cardholder’s issuing bank for the costs of providing and maintaining the payment card [...] incentivise issuing banks to promote payment cards to customers [...] cover the costs associated with network infrastructure, fraud prevention measures and other services provided by the networks.”).

34 Where pass-through is complete, changes in interchange fees primarily affect merchant pricing rather than acquirer margins. Where pass-through is incomplete, the acquirer may absorb part of the change, which would

May 21, 2026 10
d. Merchants. Changes in interchange fees also affect merchants’ acceptance decisions. For example (since the interchange fee is one component of the MSF charged by the acquirer), if the interchange fee increases and the acquirer reflects that increase fully in the MSF, the merchant’s cost of accepting commercial cards will increase. However, even where acceptance costs increase, merchants may continue to accept commercial cards where the net surplus from acceptance remains positive (e.g. due to increased sales, faster payment, or reduced receivables risk). In practice, the impact of interchange fee changes on merchant behaviour will depend not only on cost pass-through by acquirers, but also on the overall value derived from card acceptance under prevailing competitive conditions.

19. In a three-party network (such as American Express or Discover), the network operator acts as both issuer and acquirer. It manages the relationship with both the cardholder and the merchant directly. Instead of an interchange fee between separate issuing and acquiring banks, the network sets the MSF and any cardholder-facing fees directly. Although the organisational structure differs, the underlying economic challenge is similar: the platform must balance incentives to encourage both usage by cardholders and acceptance by merchants. Overall, a three-party network operates an implicit interchange fee with the acquiring side partially funding issuing costs.
20. In New Zealand, American Express is the main three-party network, while Visa and Mastercard are the main four-party networks. Other networks, such as UnionPay and JCB, are also present, but their role is more limited, with JCB acceptance facilitated through its partnership with American Express.

35 Card issuance and acquiring are concentrated among a small number of banks, including ANZ Bank, ASB Bank, Bank of New Zealand (BNZ), Westpac, and Kiwibank.

2.2 Commercial cards generate significant economic and operational benefits to both cardholders and merchants
21. Commercial cards are associated with a number of economic and operational benefits. Some accrue primarily to the cardholders (i.e., the buyer businesses), some to the merchants (i.e., supplier businesses), and some to both sides of the system. These benefits are relevant when assessing the role of the interchange fees in sustaining the commercial card ecosystem.36 The remainder of this section expands on these benefits. A systematic summary of these can be found in Table 1 at the end of this report.

2.2.1. Liquidity and working capital support for both cardholders and merchants, particularly SMEs
22. Commercial cards provide liquidity to both cardholders and merchants, by allowing cardholders to defer payment through short-term credit and by supporting merchants with faster and more reduce its net margin. The degree and symmetry of pass-through (i.e. whether increases and decreases in interchange fees are reflected similarly in merchant pricing) is an empirical question.
35 JCB, “What We Do,” available at https://www.global.jcb/en/about-us/what-we-do/index.html (“JCB cards are accepted in Australia, New Zealand, and Canada through our partnership with American Express”).
36 The commercial card ecosystem includes all participants involved in commercial card payments, including issuers, acquirers, networks, merchants, and cardholders.

May 21, 2026 11 predictable receipt of funds relative to invoice-based payment terms.

37 These timing effects may be especially relevant where firms face tighter cash-flow constraints.

23. For cardholders, commercial cards are predominantly credit-based products and are frequently used as a source of short-term financing. Unlike other payment instruments, which typically require (near) immediate settlement, commercial cards allow cardholders to access short-term liquidity at the point of transaction without the need for separate financing arrangements.38
24. Empirical evidence supports the importance of commercial cards as a financing tool: an NBER working paper finds that 55% of US businesses surveyed used commercial cards for financing in the previous 12 months, with younger and smaller firms relying more heavily on card credit.39 In the same study, over 61% of businesses reported financial benefits of commercial cards, such as ease of access, emergency use, and repayment.

40 Survey evidence similarly indicates that a significant proportion of small firms use credit cards as a financing and cash flow management tool.

25. The availability of interest-free credit associated with commercial credit cards can also generate value for cardholders. Publicly available evidence reinforces the use of commercial cards by businesses for working capital management.

May 21, 2026 12
26. For merchants, commercial card acceptance can improve working capital by accelerating receivables and reducing exposure to slow payment under invoice-based terms (e.g., a 30 or 60- day settlement period). This can allow suppliers to receive payments more quickly, regardless of the buyer's payment timing, accelerating cash conversion43 and reducing days sales outstanding.44
27. This acceleration of payment reflects an embedded financing function within card transactions.
While settlement occurs via the acquirer, the issuer’s extension of credit to the cardholder enables funds to be transferred to the merchant at or shortly after the point of sale, with the cardholder repaying at a later date (often within an interest-free period). From the merchant’s perspective, this is economically similar to receivables financing (or factoring), whereby future payment obligations are converted into immediate, guaranteed cash flows in exchange for a fee (the merchant service fee).45 This mechanism reduces reliance on invoice-based credit and external working capital facilities.

28. These merchant-side liquidity benefits are supported by global survey evidence. In one global survey of senior financial decision-makers at large B2B suppliers, card accepting merchants were
14 percentage points more likely than non-acceptors to say they were very efficient at maximising working capital and 12 percentage points less likely to say they often faced working-capital challenges.

48 in Europe, the equivalent figures were 48% and 50 basis points.49
29. This evidence suggests that working capital benefits are not confined to cardholders, but also accrue materially to merchants through faster, more predictable, and lower-risk settlement. While some merchants may offer similar nominal payment terms across instruments, commercial cards differ in the certainty and timing of payment, as well as in the reduction of collection risk and administrative burden. The relevant comparison is therefore not limited to stated payment terms, but extends to the overall quality and reliability of cash flows. These features can have a meaningful impact on working capital, particularly for SMEs and firms with limited access to alternative financing. For example, while factoring is generally more accessible for large businesses (it requires credit assessment, banking relationships, and scale), SMEs can gain access to sophisticated financing through commercial cards.

30. Commercial card acceptance may therefore be understood in economic terms as a timing tradeoff, under which the supplier receives payment earlier and with greater certainty relative to ordinary invoice terms in exchange for a transaction fee. This is particularly valuable for SMEs, who may have more limited access to traditional financing.50
2.2.2. Operational efficiency, data and cost reduction for both cardholders and merchants
31. Commercial cards can streamline payment and reconciliation processes for both cardholders and merchants. For cardholders, they can reduce administrative effort through more automated workflows, improved spend controls, and better integration with internal finance systems. For merchants, they can reduce exception handling51 and payment processing frictions by improving remittance visibility and reconciliation.

32. On the cardholder side, commercial cards are frequently integrated into accounting and enterprise resource planning systems,52 enabling automated reconciliation, streamlined expense management, and improved audit trails. Commercial cards also provide greater visibility into spending data, with 61% of cardholders citing this as a key reason for using commercial cards for B2B payments.53 This allows firms to monitor expenditure more closely, apply internal controls more effectively, and reduce manual back-office processing.

33. For cardholders, this combination of improved spend visibility, process automation, and access to short-term liquidity through card-based payments can similarly support expansion and operational resilience. By optimising working capital and reducing administrative frictions, firms are better able to manage cash flow volatility, pursue new procurement or investment opportunities, and scale operations without commensurate increases in back-office costs.54
34. More efficient and predictable payment processes can also enable firms to engage with a broader set of suppliers, including those that may previously have been considered too operationally complex or resource-intensive to manage. In this way, in some instances, the efficiency and data benefits of commercial cards can contribute not only to cost reduction, but to a reconfiguration of business models that supports growth, innovation, and more stable commercial relationships across the B2B ecosystem.55
35. On the merchant side, studies examining B2B card usage report that suppliers experience substantial reductions in administrative costs, including significant decreases in reconciliation time and dispute resolution costs relative to traditional payment instruments.

2.2.3. Enhanced fraud protection and payment security for cardholders and merchants
39. Commercial cards can enhance payment security for both cardholders and merchants through advanced fraud detection tools, virtual card65 functionality, and transaction-level controls. This is particularly relevant given the fraud risks associated with alternative payment instruments – for example, almost two thirds of firms report experiencing actual or attempted fraud via cheques.66
40. On the cardholder side, security benefits arise primarily from the ability to place tighter security around payment initiation and use. Survey evidence indicates that fraud attempts are materially lower for virtual cards (5%) relative to certain alternative payment instruments such as cheques (63%), ACH debits (38%), or wire transfers (30%).

67
41. On the merchant side, fraud protection is one of the most frequently cited reasons for adopting commercial cards.

68 A 2025 global survey of senior financial decision-makers at large B2B suppliers reported that 31% of card-accepting suppliers identified increased transaction security as a benefit of acceptance and 25% identified lower fraud incidence as a benefit.69 Enhanced controls and monitoring can be particularly valuable in multi-user business environments, where oversight and risk management requirements are more complex than in typical consumer transactions.

3.0 Commercial card interchange in New Zealand:

the case for a distinct, context-specific assessment
44. This section explains why regulatory approaches developed for other card product categories, particularly consumer cards, and in other jurisdictions cannot be assumed to apply directly to commercial cards in New Zealand. It first sets out the features of the New Zealand economy that make commercial cards particularly important for SME liquidity, cash-flow resilience, and adjustment to payment delays and external shocks (Section 3.1). It then explains why commercial cards exhibit distinct economic characteristics from consumer cards, including their stronger credit function, closer integration with broader banking relationships, higher transaction values, and greater service and control requirements (Section 3.2). Together, these considerations show that the New Zealand commercial card segment requires a distinct, market-specific assessment rather than a simple transposition of consumer-card logic or overseas regulatory settings.

71 Visa/Forrester, Drive Commercial Card Growth with Custom Interchange, p.8 (2024) (“66% [of supplier respondents] stated that they have increased access to new B2B customers and/or markets that may not be able to pay via other methods.”).

72 Visa/KoreFusion, Understanding the value of commercial card acceptance in Asia Pacific, p.4 (2025) (“Ninety-six (96%) percent of these merchants [that accept card payments] want to increase card payment volumes in order to increase sales and reduce their payment acceptance costs.”).
73 Visa/KoreFusion, Understanding the value of commercial card acceptance in Asia Pacific, p.14 (2025) (“These benefits are also observed by all suppliers with 35% of non-card accepting suppliers reporting lost sales due to non-acceptance, with these losses averaging 1.31% of their total B2B sales.”).

3.1 New Zealand’s economic structure makes commercial cards particularly important for SME liquidity and resilience
45. Several features of the New Zealand economy are relevant to the role and value of commercial cards. In particular, the predominance of small businesses in New Zealand, their exposure to payment delays in B2B transactions, and New Zealand’s vulnerability to external trade shocks as a small open economy, all provide important context for assessing the importance of commercial cards for New Zealand and any knock-on effects of regulating commercial card interchange fees.
46. Small businesses make up the overwhelming majority of New Zealand firms, so issues affecting small business cash flow are economically significant to New Zealand.75 The Ministry of Business, Innovation, and Employment (MBIE) notes that small businesses tend to be owner-operated, time-poor and budget constrained.76 In such an economy, financial tools that provide cash flow flexibility are likely to be particularly important. This is consistent with evidence from the Reserve Bank of New Zealand (RBNZ) that firms have increasingly relied on overdraft facilities for working capital as cash buffers have eroded.77 For many of these businesses, commercial cards can provide not only a means of settling transactions but also a flexible source of short-term financing.
47. Available New Zealand card data supports that commercial cards are a growing segment of the B2B payments landscape. Commercial card spend has grown year on year, from $7.2bn in 2023 to $8.2bn in 2026,
78 indicating that New Zealand businesses are increasingly adopting commercial cards as part of their payment portfolio.

48. Commercial cards’ ability to reduce administrative and payment-management frictions can also generate value for small businesses. MBIE’s research indicates that many New Zealand businesses still face practical barriers to adopting and using digital tools, including lack of time (35%), lack of staff skills (35%), and lack of knowledge about which tools to invest in (39%).79 In that context, commercial cards can reduce the time, effort and in-house capabilities required for

They tend to be owner-operated and have limited access to external support […] Their owners are time-poor and tend to wear many hats […] They face budget constraints.”).
77 RBNZ, “Financial Stability Report,” p.19 (November 2025), available at https://www.rbnz.govt.nz/- /media/project/sites/rbnz/files/publications/financial-stability-reports/2025/november/fsr-nov-2025.pdf (“Where cash buffers have eroded, firms may rely on banks to provide working capital. Since the start of 2021, businesses have borrowed more through overdraft facilities.”).
