May 21, 2026 19 small businesses to manage spending, reconcile transactions and administer payments efficiently.

49. Small businesses in New Zealand are also exposed to delayed payments by their business customers. MBIE notes that trade credit is common in B2B transactions, meaning that suppliers often deliver first and are paid later under invoice terms.80 If business customers then pay late, or impose lengthy payment terms, the supplier may face cash-flow pressure, disruption to hiring or investment, and a need for additional financing.81 MBIE further notes that these effects tend to fall disproportionately on small businesses.82 In that setting, commercial cards may have particular value as a payment tool that can support liquidity and reduce exposure to delayed payments.

50. New Zealand’s macroeconomic characteristics further shape the relevance of commercial cards, particularly for small businesses. New Zealand is a small, open economy with a high dependence on imports and exports. The RBNZ notes that, as a small economy, New Zealand is vulnerable to global shocks and spillovers,83 and that heightened global uncertainty can affect decision making by New Zealand firms.84 World Bank data likewise indicates that New Zealand’s economy is highly exposed to trade, with imports of goods and services equal to 26.7% of GDP and exports equal to 23.7% of GDP in 2023.85 Recent SME-focused evidence suggests that conditions for New Zealand small businesses remained challenging heading into 2026.86
80 MBIE, “Regulatory Impact Statement: Better Business to Business Payment Practices,” p.4 (2 December 2021), available at https://www.mbie.govt.nz/assets/regulatory-impact-statement-better-business-to-businesspayment-practices.pdf (“It is common practice for businesses to supply goods and services on trade credit to their business customers. Trade credit is where payment is deferred for a period after delivery, for example, within 30 days of the invoice date or by the 20th of the month following the invoice date, rather than requiring immediate payment.”).

81 MBIE, Better Business to Business Payment Practices, p.4 (2021) (“The impacts of late and lengthy payments include: disruption to hiring and capital investment due to uncertain cashflow, a need to source additional financing, and an increased risk of insolvency in the case of already capital constrained firms.”).
82 MBIE, Better Business to Business Payment Practices, p.4 (2021) (“Where there is harm from such practices, it tends to fall disproportionately on small businesses. Small businesses are less resilient to poor payment practices because they are less well-equipped or resourced to weather such practices.”).
83 RBNZ, “Opening the toolbox: how does the Reserve Bank analyse the world?,” (1 May 2019), available at https://www.rbnz.govt.nz/hub/publications/bulletin/2019/rbb2019-82-04 (“As a small open economy, New Zealand is particularly vulnerable to international shocks […] These events, or shocks as they are called by economists, can either affect many countries simultaneously (i.e. a global shock), or originate in one country and transmit to other countries (i.e. spillovers).”).

84 RBNZ, Analytical note on macroeconomic uncertainty (2025) (“Since New Zealand is a small open economy, heightened global uncertainty can influence sentiment and decision-making for New Zealand firms and households.”).

85 See World Integrated Trade Solution (World Bank), “New Zealand Trade,” (2023), available at https://wits.worldbank.org/countrysnapshot/en/NZL/textview.

86 Only 38% of New Zealand small businesses recorded growth in 2025, and 48% expected growth in 2026, the lowest rates among the 11 surveyed Asia-Pacific markets. May 21, 2026 20
51. In such an environment, the ability of firms – particularly small businesses – to preserve liquidity and quickly adjust to changing trade conditions (for example, in response to cost shocks such as increases in fuel prices, or disruptions to supply chains that raise procurement costs and create cash flow pressures) is critical. In such conditions, access to flexible sources of short-term financing becomes increasingly important. Rather than supporting risk-taking or expansionary investment, products such as commercial cards can enable businesses to manage liquidity, smooth cash flow, and respond to short-term fluctuations in demand and costs, thereby supporting resilience.

52. Overall, New Zealand’s combination of an SME-heavy business base, widespread use of trade credit, and exposure to external shocks give commercial cards the potential to be a broader economic function rather than just a simple payment execution tool. This means that interchange fee regulation that affects the availability or functionality of commercial cards may indirectly affect SME resilience and financial flexibility.

3.2 Commercial cards exhibit distinct economic characteristics relative to consumer cards
53. The existence of consumer card interchange fee regulation does not imply that the same analytical framework can be applied to commercial cards. Commercial cards differ materially in their usage patterns, risk characteristics, and institutional features. As set out below, these differences mean that the economics of consumer card markets cannot simply be transposed to the commercial segment.

54. The commercial card segment demonstrates a significantly higher reliance on credit and overall reliance on credit compared to the consumer segment.

87 Approximately 97% of commercial cards are credit cards, compared with a substantially lower share (60%) in the consumer segment.

88 Average commercial card limits for SMEs are materially higher than consumer limits (for example, around USD 16,000 in the United States), with an average utilisation rate of around 26%.

89 The greater reliance on credit in the commercial segment has implications for issuer risk exposure, capital allocation, and product design.

55. Unlike for consumer cards, businesses in New Zealand use commercial cards as a shortterm liquidity tool rather than a long-term source of revolving debt. In fact, commercial card balances remain low at roughly 6% of total commercial card spend, compared with consumer In response to this environment, SMEs are adapting cautiously, with a focus on cost control and financial flexibility rather than expansion. CPA Australia, Asia-Pacific Small Business Survey: New Zealand, p.3 (2026) ("Most businesses are unlikely to pursue high-growth or expansion strategies").

87 We define credit intensity as the extent of credit exposure, reflected in the magnitude of balances or credit limits relative to spend.

88 Mastercard/Oxera, The economics of commercial cards, p.1 (2019) (“Commercial cards are a potential source of credit, and this is reflected in Mastercard’s mix of cards in Europe: 60% of consumer cards are credit cards, compared with 97% of commercial cards.”).

89 Benetton and Buchak, “Revolving Credit to SMEs: The Role of Business Credit Cards,” p.4 (13 December 2024), available at https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=4997456 (“[W]e document that the average limit on business cards is roughly $16 thousand with an average utilization rate of around 26%.”).
The utilisation rate refers to the percentage of a borrower’s available credit limit that is currently being used on revolving credit accounts such as credit cards. See Citi, “What Is a Credit Utilization Rate?,” (29 October 2025), available at https://www.citi.com/credit-cards/understanding-credit-cards/what-is-credit-utilization-ratio.

May 21, 2026 21 card balances of approximately 12% of total consumer card spend.

90 This functionality differentiates commercial card network incentives from consumer cards; commercial cardholders repay balances quickly leading to low interest revenue for issuers. Issuers then rely more on transaction-linked revenue, to maintain commercial card offerings.

56. In contrast to consumer cards, commercial card programmes are often embedded within broader banking relationships, enabling the provision of more flexible financing for businesses. This can include overdrafts, lending facilities, and treasury services. Issuers rely on ongoing relationships with business customers to assess credit risk, adjust limits in line with revenue growth, and integrate card facilities with other forms of financing. This relationship-based model differs from the more standardised nature of consumer card issuance. In this context, interchange fees may play a more significant role in the commercial card market in supporting issuer participation and investment.91 On the merchant side, this financing capability can complement or substitute for trade credit. By contrast, commercial cards enable suppliers to receive payment promptly,92 while the issuer assumes the credit exposure to the buyer.93
57. B2B transactions frequently occur within ongoing supplier-buyer commercial relationships, in contrast to consumer transactions, which are typically arm’s length. As a result, merchant acceptance decisions may be influenced not only by the cost of acceptance,94 but also by the importance of maintaining commercial relationships with key buyers.95 This is supported by merchant research: almost two-thirds of small businesses surveyed identified ease of payment for customers as the most important factor when adopting a new payment instrument, while around one third cited access to a new customer base as a benefit of adopting new payment instruments.

May 21, 2026 22 commercial card acceptance and hence may be less important for the acceptance than in consumer card markets.

58. Commercial card transactions tend to be larger on average than consumer transactions, leading to greater exposure per account for issuers.

97 While commercial cards typically represent a smaller share of total card transactions by volume than consumer cards,98 they account for a disproportionately higher share of transaction value. Higher transaction values and higher credit limits imply greater exposure per account for issuers and, correspondingly, higher potential risk and servicing costs. These features result in a more concentrated and, in many cases, more commercially sensitive risk profile compared to the diversified exposures characteristic of consumer card portfolios. For merchants, larger transaction values can increase the importance of payment certainty and timely settlement, particularly where invoice-based alternatives would otherwise expose them to higher counterparty risk or delayed cash flows.99
59. Commercial cardholders often require functionality beyond basic payment capability.
Merchants may also benefit from these enhanced features through improved payment visibility,100 richer remittance data,101 and more efficient reconciliation processes102 compared to traditional invoicing arrangements. This reflects the nature of B2B transactions, which are often higher value and more volatile than consumer transactions, creating the need for additional services. These services can include:

a. Integration with corporate accounting platforms. In New Zealand, commercial card functionality often extends beyond payment capability to include integration with expense management, corporate accounting, and reimbursement processes.103 This can help address common challenges faced by New Zealand small businesses in paying supplier retaining more business (28%) […] were some of the other benefits realised by small businesses from adopting new payment methods in their business in the last 6 to 12 months.”).

60. These features reflect the fact that commercial cards are frequently used as part of a broader payments and financial management solution, rather than solely as a retail payment instrument.
Commercial cards provide value in business-payment contexts where cardholders value working capital flexibility, internal controls, and integration with finance, procurement, or expensemanagement processes.112
61. Taken together, these characteristics indicate that commercial cards serve distinct economic functions compared to consumer cards. These structural differences are directly relevant when assessing the competitive dynamics and appropriate regulatory framework for the commercial card segment. The distinction between commercial and consumer cards is also reflected in regulatory practice overseas.

62. In the EU, the Interchange Fee Regulation (IFR) capped interchange fees for consumer debit and consumer credit cards, but commercial cards were treated separately.113 The United Kingdom’s inherited IFR framework likewise continues to only cap consumer debit and credit transactions, excluding commercial cards.

4.0 Competitive dynamics and alternatives in the
B2B payments ecosystem
64. This section provides an overview of the broader B2B payments ecosystem within which commercial cards operate, including the range of alternative payment instruments available to businesses and the evolving competitive landscape. It first describes the different payment instruments used in B2B transactions, and how commercial cards interact with and differ from other instruments such as bank transfers, cheques, and emerging fintech-enabled solutions (Section 4.1). It then considers the dynamic nature of competition in B2B payments, including the role of ongoing innovation, open banking developments, and the entry of new providers in expanding the set of options available to businesses (Section 4.2). Finally, it considers the importance of maintaining competitive neutrality between four-party networks and three-party networks, and the potential for interchange regulation to affect competitive dynamics and substitution between payment systems (Section 4.3).

65. Together, this section shows that commercial cards operate in a competitive and evolving ecosystem, in which businesses have access to a range of alternative payment instruments and solutions.

4.1 Commercial cards operate within a broader B2B payments ecosystem
66. B2B payments are made using a range of instruments. These include card payments and account-to-account bank transfers (e.g., bank transfers or faster/instant payment systems), alongside other payment instruments such as cheques. Global payment studies note that B2B payments are still dominated by bank transfers and instant payments, even in the face of widespread digital adoption.117 Asia-Pacific centric studies also note that electronic funds transfer dominate B2B payments, while also noting that cash remains in use in some emerging markets.118
67. This broader picture is consistent with Mastercard’s estimate of a global commercial payments opportunity of approximately $80 trillion, of which around $77 trillion are non-carded payments.
For commercial point-of-sale transactions, cash and cheque remain significant globally: of an estimated $17 trillion (of the $80 trillion market), around $16 trillion (94%) is paid by cash or cheque, compared with around $1 trillion (6%) by card. Invoice-based payments account for around $63 trillion (out of the $80 trillion market), of which approximately $53 trillion (84%) is paid
116 RBA Conclusions Paper, p.54 (2026) (“Commercial credit cards are similar products to consumer credit cards. Both products serve similar purposes of facilitating payments and smoothing consumption…”)
117 McKinsey, “The 2025 McKinsey Global Payments Report: Competing Systems, Contested Outcomes,” (26 September 2025), available at https://www.mckinsey.com/industries/financial-services/our-insights/globalpayments-report (“Digital adoption is widespread in B2B payments; however, activity is mainly in low-margin channels such as bank transfers and instant payments.”).

118 Boost Payment Solutions, “State of B2B Payments: Asia Pacific,” p.2, available at https://www.boostb2b.com/hubfs/APAC%20White%20Paper.pdf (“Within individual countries, electronic funds transfer is the predominant means of B2B payments in most of the markets. There is still cash flowing around emerging markets as well.”).

May 21, 2026 26 by account-to-account bank transfers, around $8 trillion (13%) by cheque, and around $2 trillion (3%) by card.119
68. Commercial cards typically operate alongside other payment channels, including bank transfers, across a range of payment categories. We note that there are a number of alternative payment instruments available to commercial cardholders. In broad terms, these alternatives differ in the extent to which they replicate (i) the ability to pay and (ii) any operational tools that may be bundled with commercial card programmes (e.g., expense workflows and controls).

a. Cash. For some use cases, such as small, infrequent purchases, office expenses or for very small businesses who largely receive cash payments, cash would be a viable alternative to commercial cards.120
b. Cheques and bank transfers. Businesses also use bank and wire transfers and cheques for at least some of their payment needs.121
c. Three-party network operators. Three-party networks, like American Express, can provide an alternative card-based payment option wherein the network provides both issuing and acquiring services.122
d. Consumer credit and debit cards. Microbusinesses with very few employees could use personal consumer credit and debit cards instead of commercial cards.123 Personal cards may be used for business expenses because they are already available and provide immediate access to funds.124 However, personal cards are not equivalent to commercial cards, which are specifically designed for business use and typically provide enhanced controls, richer transaction data, and integration with accounting systems. Moreover, using

122 Irish Legal, “Three party card scheme may be tantamount to four party scheme for interchange fee purposes,” (14 February 2018), available at https://www.irishlegal.com/articles/three-party-card-scheme-maytantamount-four-party-scheme-interchange-fee-purposes (“In contrast, in three party schemes, the acquiring and issuing services are provided by the scheme itself and card-based payment transactions are made from the payment account of a payer to the payment account of a payee within the scheme. American Express operates a three party payment card scheme.”).

123 For example, research commissioned by Mastercard finds that 30% of SMEs use personal credit cards for business expenses. 124 Funding Circle, “Can I use a personal credit card for business?,” available at https://www.fundingcircle.com/uk/resources/business-finance/can-i-use-a-personal-credit-card-for-business/ (“If you’re just starting out or need immediate payment options, using your personal card requires no additional applications or waiting periods.”).

May 21, 2026 27 personal cards for business purposes can create risks, including personal liability, reduced cost efficiency, and a higher likelihood of accounting errors and tax complications.125
a. Fintech solutions.126 Increasingly, businesses also use fintech-enabled payment and spend-management tools. These products sit at different layers of the payments stack. Some operate as interface solutions, such as digital wallets, which change how payments are initiated or presented while continuing to rely on an underlying card or bank account. Others provide digital or API-based functionality, such as foreign exchange services, virtual cards, or spend-management tools. A further group comprises account-to-account and openbanking solutions, which enable payments to be made directly from bank accounts without using card rails. These products illustrate the growing range of digital payment and paymentmanagement tools available to businesses. Examples from the New Zealand market are discussed below.

69. Businesses commonly use more than one payment instrument.127 The instrument choice depends on the spend context and the operating needs around that spend (e.g., timing, controls, and credit). For example, bank transfers are often used when payment follows an invoice or agreed settlement terms. On the other hand, commercial cards are often used when firms want stronger controls, reporting, and simpler administration around spend.

70. In New Zealand, these alternative payment instruments appear in different forms and remain relevant to different business contexts. Bank transfers and other account-to-account payments remain an important part of the wider business payments landscape. This is reinforced by the RBNZ’s recent focus on improving New Zealand’s real-time account-to-account payment capability.128 Three-party networks, such as American Express, also operate as an alternative card-based option.

71. Cash also remains relevant in the New Zealand payments landscape, especially to small businesses. Recent survey evidence indicates that 72% of small businesses would be adversely
125 Ramp, “Can you use a personal credit card for business expenses?,” (14 July 2025), available at https://ramp.com/blog/using-personal-credit-card-for-business-expenses (“Using a personal credit card for business can make it harder to keep business and personal spending separate, cause bookkeeping difficulties, and open you up to personal liability if your business runs into financial trouble.”).
126 We define fintech solutions as digital tools and platforms that help businesses execute, manage, and optimise payments and payment-related operations across the value chain (examples include wallets, FX tools, spend management platforms, amongst others). See House of Commons Library, “Fintech: a guide to financial technology,” (26 April 2021), available at https://researchbriefings.files.parliament.uk/documents/CBP9150/CBP-9150.pdf.

May 21, 2026 28 affected if cash were unavailable as a means of payment.129 RBNZ evidence also indicates that over 80% of adults use cash at least sometimes, 56% store cash, and 8% rely on cash as their sole means of payment.130 At the smaller end of the market, some microbusinesses may continue to use personal consumer debit or credit cards for business-related spending, particularly where formal programme features are less important.

72. From the merchant perspective, the alternative to accepting commercial cards is typically to require payment via bank transfer/ACH (or cheque) and to maintain existing invoicing and collections processes. Evidence suggests that many merchants view commercial card acceptance as a value-add relative to these alternatives, once impacts beyond just the MSF are considered.131
73. These merchant-side value drivers indicate that acceptance decisions are not driven by up-front acceptance charges alone. Switching to alternatives like bank transfers and cheques involves trade-offs, including foregone sales or higher internal costs on invoicing. As a result, evaluating alternatives to commercial cards on the merchant side requires considering both direct costs of acceptance and wider commercial and operational implications of shifting payment instruments.
Section 5.2 examines these dynamics further, including the potential implications of introducing
an interchange fee cap on merchant economics.

4.2 B2B payments are characterised by dynamic competition and ongoing innovation
74. Competition in B2B payments is dynamic and evolving over time. The competitive landscape is an area which the NZCC has stated it is particularly focused on, with a goal of supporting the entry and growth of alternative payment providers to drive greater competition in the payments market, including to card payments.132 In its 2024 submission on open banking progress in New Zealand, the NZCC stated that the development and adoption of “new, safe, low cost and accessible open banking payment solutions” could provide viable alternatives to traditional payment instruments.

133 NZCC’s recent updates and consultation documents indicate that open banking has remained an active area of regulatory and policy focus in New Zealand.

4.3 Flexible, unregulated interchange maintains competitive neutrality between four-party networks and three-party networks
81. Interchange regulation may create competitive distortions where it constrains four-party networks (such as Mastercard and Visa) but does not apply in the same way to three-party networks (such as American Express).

82. In this context, regulation could alter the relative attractiveness and competitive position of different card-based payment options for commercial card transactions. Importantly, as discussed further in Section 5.2, economic theory suggests that cardholders and merchants would adjust their behaviour and payment choices in response to the introduction of a cap. If lower interchange reduces revenues for Mastercard and Visa issuers and, in turn, impacts issuers’ ability to support a robust commercial card offering and related features, cardholders may have stronger incentives to switch to three-party networks for at least some commercial spend.

83. As outlined in Sections 2.2 and 3.2, commercial cardholders often value features beyond basic payment execution, including working-capital flexibility, expense-management functionality, programme servicing, and integrated controls. To the extent that lower interchange reduces the ability of four-party issuers to support these features, commercial cardholders may have stronger incentives to shift spend toward alternative networks that continue to offer a more attractive commercial card proposition.

84. This position is also shared by the RBAin their Conclusions Paper, in which the Payments System Board (PSB) recognised that reducing interchange fees on commercial credit cards could harm competition in the commercial card market, and that there is a material risk that such reductions could confer a competitive advantage on three-party networks that are not subject to equivalent interchange regulation, such as American Express.151
85. The RBA has also stated that, in Australia, American Express cards are more expensive to merchants to accept than equivalent four-party network cards.152 In particular, if regulation were
151 RBA Conclusions Paper, p.55 (2026) (“However, the PSB has concluded that reducing interchange on commercial credit cards at this time would harm competition for commercial credit cards, which would likely offset any efficiency benefits of lower interchange fees. The PSB considers that there is a substantial risk that such reductions could materially advantage three-party networks that are not subject to interchange regulation, given the current competitive dynamics, where American Express is by far the largest issuer of commercial credit cards. This would likely raise payment costs for merchants.”).

152 RBA Conclusions Paper, p.55 (2026) (“This could raise payment costs because domestic-issued American Express cards are on average more expensive for Australian merchants to accept than equivalent four-party network cards.”). Recall that in four-party networks, interchange is paid by acquirers to issuers. In three-party networks, there is no equivalent explicit interchange fee because the network performs both issuing and acquiring functions. However, three-party networks still charge merchants an MSC [or MSF], which funds the costs of serving both sides of the platform and balancing cardholder incentives with merchant acceptance.

May 21, 2026 32 to encourage substitution away from regulated four-party commercial cards toward unregulated three-party networks, merchants could face higher overall acceptance costs despite lower regulated interchange fees within four-party systems. While there is no reliable publicly available information on Amex’s share of the market in New Zealand, we understand that they are a material competitor to the four-party networks.

5.0 The role of interchange fees in sustaining participation and investment in commercial cards
86. Commercial cards operate as two-sided platforms – the more merchants (i.e., supplier businesses) that accept a particular commercial card, the greater the benefits cardholders (i.e., buyer businesses) receive from holding the card and vice versa. The two-sidedness of the market results in there being strong value-generating interdependencies between market participants.153
87. This section considers how interchange fees shape the economic incentives of each participant in the commercial card ecosystem (5.1). It then examines the broader coordination function that interchange fees perform in sustaining participation and balancing value across the commercial card ecosystem and evaluates how a cap on commercial cards may alter incentives across the ecosystem (5.2).

88. Our framework suggests that:

a. A reduction in the interchange fee would reduce the ability and incentive for issuers to offer Mastercard and Visa commercial card network value-added services.

b. A reduction in the supply and quality of Mastercard and Visa card offerings would incentivise cardholders to switch to other card networks (e.g., American Express) and other payment instruments.

c. This, in turn, would force merchants to increasingly accept alternative payment instruments that can be more costly to them. For example, an EU study comparing the costs of accepting different payment instruments estimates that cheques and bank transfers can be 35-110% more costly than commercial cards.154
89. Building on this analysis, Section 5.3 reviews international regulatory practice and shows that the most relevant overseas comparators support maintaining greater flexibility for commercial card interchange fees than for consumer cards.

153 Rysman, “The Economics of Two-Sided Markets,” Journal of Economic Perspectives, p.126 (2009), available at https://www.aeaweb.org/articles?id=10.1257/jep.23.3.125 (“Generally speaking, research in twosided markets explores choices by market intermediaries, particularly pricing, when there is some kind of interdependence or externality between groups of agents that the intermediary serves.”).
154 Mastercard/Oxera, The economics of commercial cards, p.1 (2019) (“[T]he estimated full costs of acceptance of cheque and bank transfer are approximately 35–110% higher than those of commercial cards.”).

May 21, 2026 33
5.1 Commercial card networks are two-sided platforms in which pricing affects participation on both sides
90. As recognised in the economic literature on payment systems and platform markets, interdependence between the two sides of the market gives rise to indirect network effects, where the attractiveness of participation on each side therefore depends, in part, on the value and participation decisions of the other side.155
91. Because of these cross-side linkages, the overall performance of the platform depends not only on the total level of fees charged within the system, but also on the structure of those fees across participants.156 Economic theory distinguishes between the price level (the aggregate prices charged by the platform for participation and use) and the price structure (how the economic value generated by the platform is allocated between the two sides).157 In two-sided platforms, adjustments to pricing on one side may affect usage, investment, and participation on the other side in ways that are not observed in one-sided markets.

92. In four-party networks, interchange fees serve as a mechanism through which value is balanced and allocated between the issuing side and the acquiring side of the platform. By transferring value from acquirers (and indirectly merchants) to issuers (and cardholders), interchange fees influence the relative prices faced by merchants and commercial cardholders, and thereby the distribution of value between the two sides of the platform. Changes in interchange fees therefore affect issuer revenues per transaction and the net value proposition for merchants, which in turn may influence credit provision, service levels, cardholder pricing, and acceptance decisions.
93. The way in which this value is shared between the two sides may affect incentives to participate.
A greater share of value accruing to issuers may support growth in cardholder adoption, credit provision, fraud management, customer servicing, and investment in functionality. At the same time, the value proposition for merchants remains central to acceptance decisions. Changes in interchange fees that alter this balance may therefore influence both the extent of issuer-side investment and the attractiveness of acceptance to merchants. In this context, flexibility in interchange fees allows the allocation of value between the two sides of the platform to adjust in response to changing market conditions, supporting efficient participation and investment decisions on both sides rather than distorting the underlying economics of the system.
94. These dynamics are particularly relevant in the commercial card segment. As discussed in Section 3.2, commercial cards are characterised by greater reliance on credit, higher transaction values, more concentrated exposure per account, and enhanced service requirements relative to consumer cards. Moreover, the commercial card market is less mature and has lower penetration
155 Huynh et al., “Explaining the Interplay Between Merchant Acceptance and Consumer Adoption in Two-Sided Markets for Payment Methods,” p.ii (August 2019), available at https://www.bankofcanada.ca/2019/08/staffworking-paper-2019-32/ (“The market for payment cards is inherently two-sided. Consumers benefit from increased merchant acceptance of payment cards and vice versa.”).

156 Rochet & Tirole, “Two-Sided Markets: A Progress Report,” p.21 (2006) (“We define two-sided markets as ones in which the price structure [...] affects the economic outcome (volume, profits, and/or welfare).”).
157 Rochet & Tirole, “Two-Sided Markets: A Progress Report,” p.6 (2006) (“We therefore introduce a distinction between the price level, defined as the total price charged by the platform to the two sides, and the price structure, referring to the decomposition or allocation of the total price between the buyer and the seller.”).

May 21, 2026 34 than the consumer card market,158 requiring greater investment by issuers to support adoption and market expansion; interchange fees may help support these investments by covering a portion of issuer costs. Issuer participation in the commercial segment is typically more capitalintensive and more closely linked to credit risk management and ongoing relationship servicing.
In this context, pricing structures in two-sided platforms imply that issuer revenues are expected to positively influence credit availability, value-added services, and participation decisions within the commercial card ecosystem.

95. These considerations are central to the proper assessment of commercial card markets, yet have not always been fully reflected in regulatory approaches. In particular, some analyses (which will be discussed in Section 5.3) have placed greater emphasis on one side of the market, without fully accounting for the interdependence between participants. It is therefore important to adopt a two-sided analytical framework that captures the underlying economics of the system when evaluating the interchange fees.

5.2 Interchange fees play a key role in shaping participation incentives in commercial cards
5.2.1. Issuers: incentives to supply credit, invest, and sustain participation in the market
96. As outlined in Section 3.2, in commercial cards, participation is more capital-intensive and more closely linked to credit risk management and customer-specific servicing than in mass-market consumer portfolios.

159
97. Interchange fees contribute to issuer revenue on a per-transaction basis and influence the expected return on issuing commercial cards. This affects ongoing issuer decisions and strategy about (i) willingness to supply credit, (ii) credit limits and underwriting standards, (iii) provision of value-added services, and (iv) investment and innovation in programme features. Where transaction-linked revenue declines, issuers may adjust these margins, particularly for smaller or higher-risk firms whose servicing costs are high relative to spend. The sustainability of commercial card programmes therefore depends not only on processing revenues but on the broader economic return associated with providing credit, managing risk, and delivering integrated service features.

98. Some regulatory cost studies, including the RBA’s 2025 Issuer Cost Study, attempt to measure the costs associated with issuing commercial cards.

160 While such studies provide useful data points, they are typically designed around specific regulatory objectives and defined cost
158 Mastercard, “Breaking down barriers in commercial card acceptance,” p.6 (December 2024), available at https://www.mastercard.com/content/dam/mccom/shared/news-and-trends/insights/2024/highlights-from-ajourney-to-support-women-entrepreneurs/commercial-card-acceptance-payments-paper-2024.pdf (“B2B and corporate payments are a decade or so behind those in the consumer space.”).

159 As noted in Section 3.2, for commercial cards, credit limits are materially higher and exposures more concentrated than in consumer portfolios. Additionally, commercial card programmes are often embedded within broader banking relationships and may involve bespoke servicing, integration with enterprise systems, and ongoing programme-level oversight.

160 See RBA, “Review of Merchant Card Payment Costs and Surcharging: Issuer Cost Study,” Table 1 p.6 (19 August 2025), available at https://www.rba.gov.au/payments-and-infrastructure/review-of-retail-paymentsregulation/issuer-cost-study/2025-08/pdf/issuer-cost-study.pdf.

May 21, 2026 35 constructs rather than a full economic assessment of issuer participation in commercial card markets.

99. In particular, regulatory cost frameworks often focus on identifiable network service costs such as authorisation, processing, certain fraud prevention expenses, and funding of the interest-free period. However, commercial card issuance entails a broader set of economically relevant costs.
These include credit underwriting, ongoing monitoring of larger and more concentrated exposures, capital allocation, dispute resolution and collections, programme-level administration, customer-specific integration with accounting and enterprise systems, and relationship management.

100. Many of these activities are central to the commercial value proposition but may sit outside narrowly defined “eligible” or “avoidable” cost categories used for benchmarking purposes. As a result, regulatory cost measures may not fully capture the economic resources committed by issuers to sustain commercial card programmes, particularly where those programmes involve differentiated services and higher capital intensity.

101. More broadly, the RBA’s reliance on an “eligible cost” framework abstracts from the broader set of functions supported by issuers in the commercial segment, including credit provision, risk management, customer servicing, and ongoing investment in functionality and network development. These activities are not fully captured by transaction-level cost metrics but are integral to the value generated by the system as a whole.

102. Therefore, an assessment of interchange fees in commercial card markets should adopt a comprehensive and economically grounded view of issuer costs and incentives. A narrow focus on technical network costs risks understating the broader credit, capital, and service commitments that underpin commercial card participation and investment.

161
103. Importantly, these investments also have a dynamic and forward-looking dimension. Issuers incur costs to recruit and incentivise cardholders, expand usage, and build network scale over time, which in turn enhances the value of acceptance for merchants. In this sense, part of the value generated for merchants arises over time through issuer-side investment, rather than solely at the point at which a transaction occurs. A cap on interchange fees would weaken these investment incentives by reducing the expected return on such upfront and ongoing expenditure, thereby slowing cardholder growth and usage, and ultimately reducing the scale and value of the network to merchants over time.

104. Economic logic can inform how a cap on interchange fees would affect issuer incentives by altering both the level of transaction-linked revenue and the drivers of transaction volume. This can be illustrated through a simple framework in which issuer revenue depends on both the fee level and the volume of transactions, with volume itself influenced by card features and merchant acceptance.

161 The academic literature has documented the impact of interchange regulation on investment decisions, with some studies suggesting that caps on interchange fees often fail to adequately account for their effects on investment incentives. See Reisinger & Zenger, “Interchange Fee Regulation and Service Investments” p. 22, (May 2019), available at https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S0167718719300311 (“In particular, regulation of interchange fees usually suggests caps because card associations may inefficiently reallocate costs via the interchange fee such that consumers benefit and retailers are hurt. This logic is based on a static perspective, in which investment incentives are ignored.”).

May 21, 2026 36
105. In particular, issuer interchange fee revenue can be expressed as the product of transaction volume and the effective interchange fee. Transaction volume is not fixed, but depends on factors such as the features and benefits offered to cardholders (which are partly funded by interchange fees) and the level of merchant acceptance (which depends, in part, on merchant-facing pricing).
106. In this framework, a reduction in interchange fees affects issuer revenue through three channels:
(i) an immediate reduction in revenue on existing transaction volumes; (ii) a reduction in cardholder value and usage as features and benefits adjust; and (iii) broader network effects, where changes in merchant acceptance and cardholder usage reinforce each other, which may reduce transaction volumes over time. The overall effect depends on the responsiveness of these factors, including the extent to which interchange fee changes are passed through to merchants and how both merchants and cardholders respond.

107. A high-level estimate suggests that commercial cards can generate up to approximately 15% of issuer transaction-linked revenues in New Zealand.162 Revenues are also likely to vary across different categories of commercial cardholders, including SMEs, mid-market customers, travel and entertainment users, and broader B2B accounts. The relative contribution of these segments to issuer revenues may imply a degree of cross-subsidisation across customer types. At the same time, servicing costs are unlikely to be uniform: larger or more complex enterprises may involve higher programme-level servicing, integration, and risk management costs, while smaller firms may involve higher relative credit risk.163 These differences in revenue contribution and cost intensity are relevant when assessing how a cap might affect issuer incentives across segments.
108. A cap would reduce issuer-side transaction revenue, which in turn may reduce service quality, cause card issuers to limit commercial card issuance, and/or reduce incentives to promote and support card issuance. Potential issuer responses include tighter underwriting, reduced credit limits, higher direct fees, rationalisation of service features, or reallocation toward lower-risk customers. Uniform pricing constraints may result in greater standardisation of offerings and reduced tailoring to specific business needs.

109. Over time, a cap could also result in increased barriers to entry for smaller or innovative issuers (e.g. fintech), particularly where their business models rely on interchange revenue. For these providers, interchange fees often represent a key source of funding to support customer acquisition, product development, and ongoing servicing, especially where they offer low- or zerofee products to attract users.164 A reduction in this revenue stream may compress margins and
162 This figure is an estimate based on the fact that commercial cards represent around 6% of total transaction volumes in New Zealand (see Mastercard Submission to NZCC on Retail Payment System, p.27 (September 2024)). Interchange fees on domestic commercial credit cards of up to 2.20%, which can be up to approximately 2.75x higher than consumer credit card fees (0.80%). Based on this high level approximation, one can roughly estimate that commercial cards generate up to 16.5% (6%*2.75) of issuer revenues in New Zealand. 163 See CAT, Umbrella Interchange Fee Proceedings, p.264 (2025).

164 Lithic, “What is Interchange? How Fintechs Earn Interchange Revenue from Credit and Debit Cards,” (4 April 2023), available at https://www.lithic.com/blog/interchange (“Some fintechs get most of their revenue from these [interchange] fees; others use interchange income to fund customer rewards that make their products stickier.”);

Bain & Company, “As Funding Dries Up, Can Neobanks Diversify Their Revenue Streams?,” available at

May 21, 2026 37 make it more difficult to achieve scale, which in turn can disproportionately disadvantage smaller players relative to established incumbents with more diversified income sources and larger customer bases.

5.2.2. Cardholders (buyer businesses): incentives to use cards as both payment and financing tools
110. As Section 2.2 expands on, commercial cardholders derive value not only from payment execution but from integrated credit and spend control features.165 If issuer-side incentives weaken due to an interchange fee cap, adjustments in credit limits, pricing, or service features may affect business usage decisions.

111. Where cardholder value depends on integrated credit, liquidity management, and transaction control functions, a reduction in these services or credit availability may alter business usage decisions. If issuers respond to reduced transaction-linked revenue by tightening credit limits, increasing direct fees, or scaling back programme features (such as detailed reporting, spend controls, or integration tools), the net value proposition of commercial cards may decline for certain firms.

112. Businesses that rely on these bundled features for working capital management or internal control processes may therefore consider alternative payment and financing arrangements. This in turn has the potential to negatively affect merchant economics via broader network effects, where changes in merchant acceptance and cardholder usage reinforce each other.

5.2.3. Acquirers: incentives to pass through interchange and expand merchant acceptance
113. Acquirers provide a bundle of merchant services supporting the full transaction lifecycle, including onboarding and underwriting, transaction authorisation, clearing and settlement, and the provision of information and reporting statements.166 They also undertake risk management functions, such as assuming chargeback risk and conducting compliance checks (e.g. know your https://www.bain.com/insights/as-funding-dries-up-can-neobanks-diversify-their-revenue-streams/ (“To turn customer acquisition into revenue, neobanks have traditionally relied heavily on interchange fees”).
165 Evidence suggests that a significant proportion of small businesses rely on business credit cards for working capital and liquidity management during the year. JPMorgan Chase Institute, Small Business Use of Credit Cards (2023) (“Credit cards are part of a suite of cash flow management tools. Credit cards can be an alternative for cash liquidity, providing financing when firms cannot pay in cash.”).
Commercial cards often combine settlement, revolving credit, transaction controls, and reporting tools in a single instrument. Chase, “Business Credit Cards vs. Consumer Credit Cards,” available at https://www.chase.com/personal/credit-cards/education/basics/business-vs-consumer-credit-cards (“Common features of business credit cards [...] Custom rewards programs [...] Expense management tools [...] Enhanced spending controls [...] Higher credit limits”).

166 Kjos, “The Merchant-Acquiring Side of the Payment Card Industry: Structure, Operations, and Challenges,” p.3 (October 2007), available at https://www.philadelphiafed.org/-/media/frbp/assets/consumerfinance/discussion-papers/D2007OctoberMerchantAcquiring.pdf (“In support of the transaction flow, merchant acquirers generally perform four key functions: (1) signing up and underwriting merchants to accept networkbranded cards, (2) providing the means to authorize valid card transactions at client merchant locations (3) facilitating the clearing and settlement of the transactions through the payment network, and (4) providing other relevant information services, such as sending out statements.”).

May 21, 2026 38 customer and anti-money laundering checks).167 This contrasts with the business of issuing, where revenue is primarily driven by the extension of credit and the pricing of risk.
114. Acquirers charge MSFs to merchants, which generally include three main components:
interchange fees, scheme and processing fees, and acquirer net revenue. Acquirer net revenue is the portion of the MSF retained by the acquirer to cover its cost of providing payment services and its profit margin.168 Acquirers commonly recover interchange and scheme fees from merchants through the MSF.

169 The level of the MSF is determined through negotiation between the merchant and the acquirer and can vary by merchant size, with smaller businesses typically paying higher MSFs than larger merchants.170
115. As has been amply documented in the academic and industry literature, a reduction in interchange fees does not necessarily translate into an equivalent reduction in merchant charges.
Pass-through to merchants is not always complete or immediate, and the extent of pass-through can depend on the pricing model, the merchant’s bargaining position, and the level of competition in the acquiring market.

116. This is consistent with the EC’s 2020 review of their IFR, which reported that only around half of surveyed acquirers said they had passed through all interchange-fee savings to merchants, while around two thirds said they had passed through at least part of those savings.171 Evidence from other settings also points in the same direction. For example, one Spanish study estimates that a 1% reduction in interchange fees is associated in the long run with a 0.39% reduction in MSFs, suggesting partial rather than full pass-through.172
117. The implication is that lower interchange fees do not necessarily deliver the full expected benefit to merchants. Part of any reduction may instead be retained within the acquiring side of the system and larger merchants, depending on merchant pricing arrangements and competitive conditions.

167 PSR, Card-acquiring services market review, p.21 (2021) (“The chargeback process gives rise to a credit risk for acquirers [...] Acquirers carry out due diligence on merchants as part of the onboarding process and on an ongoing basis to help them manage the credit risk and other risks they carry. For example, acquirers carry out know your customer and anti-money laundering checks.”).

168 PSR, Card-acquiring services market review, p.74 (2021) (“The MSC [or MSF] comprises: interchange fees, paid by the acquirer to the issuer [...] scheme fees, paid by the acquirer to the operator of the card payment system [...] acquirer net revenue, which recovers the acquirer’s other costs and margin”).
169 CAT, Umbrella Interchange Fee Proceedings, p.21 (2025) (“Acquirers pass on all of the MIF [...] and the scheme fee, to merchants through the MSC [or MSF]”).

170 See PSR, Card-acquiring services market review, p.64 (2021) (“Irrespective of the pricing option a large merchant has, they generally pay lower MSCs [or MSFs] for card-acquiring services than small and mediumsized merchants.”)
171 EC, Study on the application of the IFR, p.156 (2020) (“Acquirers were asked whether and how they had passed-on savings in the interchange fees to merchants. Half of the respondents indicated that they had passed-on all interchange fee savings, see Figure 66. Two thirds indicated that they had at least partially passed-on the reduction. One third indicated that they mostly compensated by improving the quality of their services.”).

172 Shabgard & Asensio, “The price effects of reducing payment card interchange fees,” p.16 (2023) (“The IF has a positive effect on the MSC [or MSF], implying that a 1% reduction in the IF is passed through as a 0.39% lower MSC [or MSF] in the long run.”).

May 21, 2026 39
5.2.4. Merchants (supplier businesses): incentives to accept and value derived from transactions
118. Merchants face MSFs that include interchange fees as a component. Reductions in interchange fees may lower acceptance costs to the extent they are passed through by acquirers. However, merchant acceptance decisions depend not only on cost but also on value generated. For instance, the previously on-the-margin merchant may realise limited or no benefit from card acceptance due to cardholders no longer receiving sufficient incentives to pay with their card.
119. The interchange fee partly reflects the value of the issuer’s immediate and guaranteed payment to the merchant. Accordingly, the MSF is often referred to as the Merchant Discount Rate (MDR), since the merchant receives payment at a discount in return for this advanced and guaranteed receipt of funds. At the same time, the issuer effectively extends credit to the cardholder and assumes repayment risk between the time of the transaction and settlement.

120. Therefore, the interchange fee should be evaluated not solely as a transactional cost, but in the context of its contribution to supporting the features that merchants value. These features – including faster, more certain payment and reduced reliance on managing receivables – are not limited to large merchants. Card acceptance can enable smaller merchants, who may have more limited access to alternative financing or sophisticated payment infrastructure, to access similar liquidity and risk-management benefits. In this sense, card systems can broaden access to payment and financing functionalities across merchants of different sizes.

121. As laid out in Sections 2 and 3, commercial card transactions are often larger in value, may settle more quickly than certain alternatives (e.g., bank transfers), and reduce counterparty and fraud risk relative to some payment instruments. In B2B contexts, card acceptance may also facilitate procurement processes and attract business customers who rely on card-based purchasing systems. The net merchant incentive depends on the balance between acceptance cost and these value drivers.

122. If changes to interchange fees were to reduce the attractiveness of commercial card programmes and prompt cardholders to substitute towards alternative payment instruments, the net effect for merchants may not necessarily be cost-reducing. As previewed, while attention often focuses on MSFs, a broader economic assessment must consider both direct and indirect costs associated with alternative payment instruments.

123. Firstly, alternative payment instruments are not necessarily cheaper in direct terms. Merchants frequently incur processing fees, scheme fees, or acceptance costs when accepting other cardbased instruments, including three-party networks. In some cases, MSFs for alternative card networks may exceed those associated with four-party commercial card transactions.
Accordingly, substitution from one card network to another may increase, rather than reduce, direct acceptance costs.

124. Further, non-card payment instruments can impose material indirect costs on merchants. Cash and cheques, for example, involve handling, storage, security, reconciliation, and deposit costs.
They also carry higher risks of theft, fraud, and error. Even bank transfers, while often perceived as low-cost, can involve administrative burdens associated with invoice matching, reconciliation, and payment tracking, particularly in high-volume or fragmented B2B environments. Industry studies, including analysis by the European Central Bank, indicate that once labour and

May 21, 2026 40 reconciliation time are taken into account, the unit costs of cash are not consistently lower than non-cash payment instruments and can in some contexts be higher.173
125. Taken together, these considerations suggest that substitution away from commercial cards may impose higher direct or indirect costs on merchants, particularly where commercial cards currently support operational efficiencies or reduce payment risk.

126. Finally, substitution effects may extend beyond shifts to alternative payment instruments. Some businesses may instead revert to using consumer credit cards for business expenditure, as suggested by NBER research.174 More broadly, this highlights that capping interchange fees on commercial cards may reduce the attractiveness of dedicated commercial card programmes, potentially leading to substitution toward less tailored or less efficient payment solutions. Such outcomes would run counter to policy objectives aimed at supporting the adoption of payment instruments that are specifically designed for business use.

5.2.5. Platform-level coordination: critical mass and value rebalancing
127. The interchange fee serves a coordination function in this multi-party system. It supports critical mass by balancing incentives between issuance and acceptance. Because business and merchant value drivers differ (and differ from those in consumer markets), pricing flexibility allows revenue to be allocated in a way that sustains participation across heterogeneous segments.
128. Pricing flexibility also allows four-party networks to support both default interchange rates and commercially negotiated bilateral arrangements between issuers and acquirers. Bilateral arrangements can reflect the specific value exchange, commercial relationship, and risk allocation agreed between participants. Regulating interchange may reduce this flexibility by limiting the scope for parties to negotiate bespoke arrangements and tailor pricing to their particular commercial circumstances.

129. Overall, evidence from jurisdictions that have reduced interchange fees (including, for example, Poland’s reforms)

175 indicates that regulatory changes can affect acceptance network development and usage growth trajectories. While outcomes are context-specific, such evidence highlights the importance of assessing dynamic participation effects rather than focusing exclusively on static merchant cost changes.

130. In commercial card markets characterised by two-sided interdependence, higher reliance on credit, and heterogeneous business needs, the effects of a cap are likely to extend beyond immediate fee adjustments. A comprehensive assessment should therefore consider impacts on
173 European Central Bank, “Costs of retail payments – an overview of recent national studies in Europe,” p.2 (December 2022), available at https://www.ecb.europa.eu/pub/pdf/scpops/ecb.op294~8ac480631a.en.pdf (“[T]he increasing number of debit card payments, to which high fixed costs are attached, has led to lower unit costs relative to those of cash.”).

174 Akcigit et al., Credit Card Entrepreneurs, p.14 (2025) (“Business credit cards are the most commonly used financing instrument, with 55% of businesses reporting their use over the past year. Personal credit cards and lines of credit follow in use but with only half the frequency of business credit cards. Additionally, a notable 26% of respondents rely on personal cash and loans to fund their business operations. The predominant use of credit cards reported by our survey respondents aligns with previous findings on small business finance.”)
175 Mastercard/Oxera, The economics of commercial cards, p.4 (2019) (“[T]he average annual number of commercial card transactions would have been significantly higher in Poland without the interchange fee cap.”).

May 21, 2026 41 credit availability, service differentiation, issuer participation, and overall B2B payments efficiency in New Zealand.

5.3 International best practice broadly supports maintaining flexibility for commercial cards interchange
131. New Zealand’s regulatory framework currently adopts a differentiated treatment across card products and channels. On the consumer side, consumer debit and credit interchange fees are capped. In the commercial segment, domestic commercial debit is currently regulated but domestic commercial credit and foreign-issued commercial credit remain uncapped.176 All domestic prepaid cards are also currently uncapped, while from 1st May 2026, caps are in effect for foreign-issued commercial debit and foreign-issued commercial prepaid transactions.177
132. International best practice supports treating commercial cards as a distinct product category and preserving greater pricing flexibility for them than for consumer cards. This is consistent with the economic reality that, as highlighted in Section 3.2, commercial cards exhibit features that are clearly distinct from consumer cards.

133. Regulatory best practice in Europe most clearly reflects this position. Indeed, when regulating interchange fees for consumer cards, both the EU and the UK left commercial cards outside the consumer cap framework, recognising them as a separate product class with different characteristics and commercial dynamics.178 The European Commission (EC) has identified a number of structural features that distinguish commercial cards from consumer cards, including market specificity, because they serve a distinct business segment and are not substitutes for consumer cards.

179
134. The best available empirical evidence also does not support intervention for commercial cards.
In its 2020 study on the application of the IFR, the EC found no evidence of statistically significant increases in commercial card interchange fees following the introduction of interchange fee caps on consumer cards that would justify removing the commercial card carve-out.180 The EC was
176 See NZCC, “Retail Payment System: Interchange Fee Regulation for Mastercard and Visa Networks – Final Decision and Reasons Paper,” Table X1, p.7 (17 July 2025).

177 At this stage, the current submission does not assess the distinction between domestic- and foreign-issued cards or the associated cross-border considerations, and instead focuses on the treatment of commercial cards as a distinct product category to consumer cards.

178 European Commission, IFR Impact Assessment, p.56 (2013) (“They [commercial cards] could not substitute consumer credit or debit cards as they cater for a specific market segments and their needs, not for the average consumer.”).

179 EC, IFR Impact Assessment, p.56 (2013) (“They [commercial cards] could not substitute consumer credit or debit cards as they cater for a specific market segments and their needs, not for the average consumer.”).
180 EC, “Study on the application of the Interchange Fee Regulation,” p.203 (2020), available at https://copenhageneconomics.com/wp-content/uploads/2021/12/copenhagen-economics_march_ifr-report.pdf (“The hypothesis that the exclusion of commercial cards from the cap set by the IFR could lead to higher interchange fees applied to commercial card transactions is not confirmed. The average interchange fee applied on commercial cards transactions decreased across the EU, from 0.95% to 0.86% of the transaction value in the period 2015-2017 [...] However, the decrease in interchange fees applied on commercial cards was not statistically significant”).

May 21, 2026 42 also concerned that leaving commercial cards uncapped might simply shift volume and fee revenue into higher-cost products, but found no evidence to this effect.

181
135. By contrast, the UK and Australian positions seem to be based on more limited empirical evidence. The UK Payment Systems Regulator (PSR) continues not to regulate commercial cards, but in its 2025 market review, it appears to treat commercial and consumer payments as functionally similar without a robust market-specific assessment of competitive constraints in business payments. The RBA has retained a separate cap for domestic–issued commercial credit cards for now rather than exempting them, but its justification turns heavily on premises which, as discussed below, have some weaknesses.

5.3.1. The EC provides the clearest and most credible international comparator
136. As noted above, the EC deliberately excluded commercial cards from the IFR caps applied to consumer debit and credit cards, recognising that commercial cards are a distinct category and should not automatically be regulated in the same way as consumer products.

182
5.3.2. The UK PSR position
137. The PSR has not imposed equivalent regulation on commercial cards. Its 2025 market review does not appear to analyse commercial payments as a distinct market. Instead, it focuses on spontaneous consumer payments as the core segment and reasons from the fact that business payments account for only a small share of overall card transactions.

183
5.3.3. The RBA Conclusions Paper (March 2026)

138. The RBA’s March 2026 Conclusions Paper supports a cautious approach to commercial card interchange regulation.

139. Importantly, the RBA assessed commercial interchange regulation by reference to market wide effects, not only the direct effects of lower Mastercard and Visa interchange fees. As stated in Section 4.3, it concluded that reducing interchange fees could harm competition for commercial credit cards and materially advantage three-party networks that are not subject to interchange regulation. It also recognised American Express as the relevant three-party competitive constraint in this context.

140. As raised in Section 4.3, given that domestic-issued American Express cards are, on average, more expensive for Australian merchants to accept than alternative four-party network cards, interchange fee caps could lead to increased merchant payment costs instead.184 The RBA
181 EC, Study on the application of the IFR, p.200 (2020) (“The relative share of commercial cards in the total number of cards in circulation (commercial cards plus consumer debit and credit cards) remained stable across EU in 2015-2017 at around 3%, see Figure 81.”).

182 EC, IFR Impact Assessment, p.56 (2013) (“They [commercial cards] could not substitute consumer credit or debit cards as they cater for a specific market segments and their needs, not for the average consumer.”).
183 See PSR, “Market review of card scheme and processing fees: Final report,” paras 4.30 and 4.32 to 4.34 (March 2025), available at https://www.psr.org.uk/media/sogjjvl4/mr22-110-card-sp-fees-mr-final-reportpublication-redacted-mar-2025-updated.pdf.
184 RBA Conclusions Paper, p.55 (2026) (“This could raise payment costs because domestic-issued American Express cards are on average more expensive for Australian merchants to accept than equivalent four-party network cards.”).

May 21, 2026 43 therefore recognised that the relevant question is not only whether a cap would reduce Mastercard and Visa interchange fees on a per-transaction basis, but whether the cap would shift spend toward higher-cost alternatives and increase merchant costs overall.

141. The RBA’s decision also supports a separate assessment of commercial and consumer credit cards. The RBA acknowledged that interchange levels have uneven competitive effects for commercial versus consumer credit cards and lowered domestic consumer credit cap to 0.3%, while maintaining the domestic commercial credit cap at 0.8%. They argued that:

185
a. The proposed reduction would have been larger for commercial credit than for consumer credit: reducing average four-party commercial interchange from around 0.78 per cent to 0.3 per cent would have represented a materially larger fall in issuer revenue than the equivalent change for consumer credit (0.47 per cent on average to 0.3 per cent with caps).
b. Commercial credit card issuers had fewer alternative revenue sources than consumer cards, because commercial cards are typically paid off in full each month, meaning issuers earn less interest income and rely more on interchange to fund cardholder propositions.
142. Together, these factors affect issuer incentives, cardholder behaviour and the likelihood of substitution toward unregulated three-party networks. They therefore reinforce the need to assess commercial card interchange on its own terms, rather than assuming that consumer-card regulation provides a reliable guide.

143. The RBA’s broader rationale for retaining regulation on commercial card interchange fees is, however, less robust. Its reasoning appears to rest on the propositions that commercial and consumer credit cards are “similar products”, that commercial cards are must-take for merchants, and that there is no evidence issuers incur higher costs on commercial cards as a share of transaction value.186 The RBA also concluded that additional benefits of commercial cards accrue primarily to cardholders rather than merchants, that interchange fees should not be used to fund cardholder rebates or rewards, and that they had not received compelling evidence for commercial cards retaining higher interchange fees to support working-capital benefits.
187 This view is incomplete, as Section 2.2 and Table 1 below demonstrate that the benefits of commercial cards accrue to both cardholders and merchants across the platform.

144. The RBA also relies on its issuer cost study188 when considering the differences between commercial and consumer credit card costs. Whilst their study suggests that commercial card
185 RBA Conclusions Paper, p.55-56 (2026) (“[S]o a reduction in the cap to 0.3 per cent would represent a much larger percentage reduction in interchange fees for commercial credit cards compared with consumer credit cards… commercial credit card issuers may have fewer revenue sources to fund issuance.”)
186 RBA Conclusions Paper, p.54 (2026); Also see RBA, Issuer Cost Study (19 August 2025).
187 RBA Conclusions Paper, p.54 (2026) (“Additional benefits of commercial credit cards accrue to the cardholder, not the merchant […] Several issuers use commercial credit card interchange revenue to provide cash rebates or rewards to cardholders […] The PSB does not consider there is a compelling efficiency case to retain high levels of interchange on commercial credit cards so as to fund any working capital benefits.”).
188 See RBA, Issuer Cost Study (19 August 2025).

We note that it has been highlighted by other courts and regulators (in particular, the UK CAT) that the evidence base on issuer economics remains underdeveloped. For example, in the CAT Trial 1 Judgment, the Tribunal expressly noted the lack of evidence on issuer-side commercial card economics, including the actual costs of different service elements, issuers’ other revenue sources, and the extent to which additional services depend on MIF revenue. See CAT, Umbrella Interchange Fee Proceedings (2025).

May 21, 2026 44 issuance is cheaper than consumer cards, this finding is based on the RBA’s eligible cost framework, which excludes several costs that are relevant to commercial cards, including funding of the interest-free period, product development, account set-up, overheads and maintenance, rewards or other cardholder benefits. The RBA also acknowledges that aspects of commercialcard offerings including rewards and expense-management programmes influence competitive outcomes.189 These value-add features are central to the commercial card proposition, but are not fully captured by a narrow eligible-cost benchmark.

145. More fundamentally, the RBA places primary emphasis on merchant-facing charges and issuer cost metrics, but seems to give limited consideration to the two-sided nature of commercial card systems and the role of interchange fees in balancing value and incentives across participants.190 The RBA’s focus on whether specific benefits accrue directly to merchants may therefore understate that, in a two-sided platform, the allocation of value between participants does not need to mirror the incidence of direct benefits on a transaction-by-transaction basis in order to be efficient.

146. Pricing structures are designed to support participation on both sides of the market, taking into account demand conditions, elasticities, and the need to sustain investment and usage. In this context, the relevant question is not whether particular features benefit cardholders or merchants in isolation, but whether the overall allocation of value supports efficient participation and maximises the total value generated by the system. A framework that recognises these crossside effects and the role of interchange fees in balancing value across participants provides a more complete basis for assessing efficiency and value distribution in commercial card markets.
189 RBA Conclusions Paper, p.55 (2026) (“While these programs are considered outside the scope of the RBA’s eligible costs framework when assessing interchange settings in the public interest, they nonetheless play a role in influencing competitive outcomes and many issuers use interchange revenue to fund them.”).
